--- Page 9 ---
<meta>This page begins the Translator's Preface, introducing the historical and literary context of the work.</meta>

## TRANSLATOR’S PREFACE.

---

The *Ikhwanu-ṣ-ṣafā*, or “Brothers of Pure Friendship” (<note>literally, Brothers of Purity</note>), is the title of fifty-one Arabic treatises of considerable antiquity. These were the joint production of several authors—some sources say five, others ten—who lived and worked together in the closest intimacy and harmony; this shared bond is the origin of the work's title. Only one of these works—the one dealing with the contest between man and the animal kingdom regarding man's claim to supremacy—has, I believe, been translated into Urdu under the same title given to the collected fifty-one treatises in Arabic. Nevertheless, some of the remaining treatises are well worth translating as well.

The Urdu version of the *Ikhwanu-ṣ-ṣafā* has for some years held the honored position of a "test-book" for the first Civil and for the Military Interpreter’s Examination <note>These were standardized language proficiency exams for British colonial officials and officers in India</note>.

<summary>The translator introduces the "Ikhwanu-ṣ-ṣafā" as a collection of ancient Arabic treatises written by a cooperative of authors, noting that the specific section on the dispute between humans and animals is a standard text for linguistic examinations.</summary>
<keywords>Ikhwanu-ṣ-ṣafā, Brothers of Purity, Arabic literature, Urdu translation, animal rights, British colonial examinations, Interpreter's Examination</keywords>

--- Page 10 ---
<meta>This page continues the Translator's Preface, discussing the academic context and methodology of the translation.</meta>

<header>
# TRANSLATOR'S PREFACE
</header>

and for the Examination for a Certificate of High Proficiency in Urdu.” It was, therefore, judged that a careful translation of it into English would prove of the highest utility to students.

In undertaking this translation, I have steadily endeavored to keep two objects in view: 
1. To facilitate the study of the original; 
2. To produce a work which might be read and appreciated by those readers who possess no knowledge of Urdu, for the interest of the story itself. 

The text of the Urdu translator has therefore been rendered throughout as closely as is consistent with clarity and due regard to the idiom of the English language.

As an additional aid to accuracy, I have, in the course of translating, compared the Urdu with the original Arabic page by page throughout. This has not only afforded me a clear insight into the real meaning of many a doubtful passage, but it has enabled me to detect many important errors in the Urdu translation—errors which disfigure the work to a degree that makes it extremely desirable that it should be subjected to careful revision and correction.

The truth is, that <term>Maulavi</term><note>A title for a Muslim doctor of law or a teacher of Arabic and Islamic studies.</note> Ikram Ali's knowledge

<summary>The translator explains that this English version aims to assist students of Urdu while remaining engaging for general readers, noting that he corrected several errors in the Urdu text by comparing it to the original Arabic.</summary>

<keywords>Urdu, Arabic, translation methodology, Maulavi Ikram Ali, language examinations, linguistics</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the translator's critique of Maulavi Ikram Ali's Urdu translation, specifically focusing on how his lack of Arabic proficiency led to significant errors and misrepresentations of Islamic scientific knowledge.</meta>

<language>English</language>
<page-type>preface</page-type>
<page-num>V</page-num>
<header>
# TRANSLATOR’S PREFACE
</header>

...in general, and his knowledge of Arabic in particular, were so poor that they completely disqualified him for the work he had the boldness <note>original: "hardiness"</note> to undertake. It is for this reason that so much nonsense and so many absurdities appear in his translation. I feel it is necessary to say that I am not judging his work as if it were a *literal* translation, but rather as a work claiming "to give the substance of the original." 

Now, he is so far from accomplishing even this that, through his ignorance of Arabic, he repeatedly turns good sense into nonsense. He either alters the meaning of the Arabic into something quite the opposite or adds details that are completely irrelevant to the subject at hand. Furthermore, in those parts of the work that touch on natural history, he has done everything in his power to make the entire body of scientific knowledge held by the Muslim <note>original: "Mohammedan"</note> people seem completely contemptible. 

These are not merely my own assertions. Any qualified judge can satisfy himself by comparing the Urdu work with the original; and the reader will find numerous instances that I have pointed out in the notes to my translation.

Regarding the work, however, simply as an Urdu...

--- Page 12 ---
<meta>This page concludes the translator's assessment of the Urdu version's literary style and introduces the specific source text used for this English edition, noting its flaws.</meta>

<language>English</language>
<page-type>preface</page-type>
<page-num>vi</page-num>
<header>
# TRANSLATOR'S PREFACE
</header>

As a literary composition, it ranks deservedly high, though in my opinion, it is far inferior to the *Bāgh-o-Bahār* <note>The *Bāgh-o-Bahār* ("Garden and Spring") is a famous Urdu classic written by Mir Amman in the early 1800s.</note>. The style is stiff and labored, and there is a lack of connection between the sentences which destroys their flow <note>original: "symmetry"</note>. This often makes it difficult for the reader to discover where one sentence ends and another begins. I am inclined <note>original: "disposed"</note> to attribute this to the defects mentioned on the previous page, for these issues are not found in his own Preface.

This English translation is based on the text edited by Major Nassau<gloss>u</gloss> Lees <note>Major William Nassau Lees (1825–1889) was a British officer and noted scholar of Oriental languages.</note>. In some respects, it is the best text available, yet it does not reflect well on the editor. It contains numerous errors, mainly typographical <note>Errors made during the printing process.</note>, which could have been easily avoided by a careful examination of the proof-sheets. Some, however, are errors in the placement of diacritical points <note>Marks added to letters to indicate specific pronunciations or to distinguish between different words.</note>. These are likely to do serious harm to any student who reads the work with an ordinary Indian *Munshi* <note>original: *Munshī*; a teacher of languages or a secretary.</note>. A really good text—one where the many gross errors of the Urdu translator are corrected—remains a great need <note>original: "desideratum"</note>.

<summary>The translator evaluates the literary style of the Urdu text and critiques the editorial flaws in Major Nassau Lees' version, which served as the basis for this translation.</summary>
<keywords>Urdu literature, Bāgh-o-Bahār, Major Nassau Lees, translation errors, diacritical points, Munshi, editing quality</keywords>

# PREFACE

Praise beyond measure is due to that Self-Existent <note>original: *Wājibu-l-wujūd*; a philosophical and theological term for a Being whose existence is necessary, self-derived, and independent.</note> Being. He gave various forms to all created bodies, even though the matter they are composed of is the same. By organizing the human species <note>The author uses the logical terms *genus* and *differentia* (distinguishing characteristics) to describe how God created humanity as a unique category of being by joining a specific essence to a general form.</note>, He gave distinct powers to every individual.

Infinite praise is right and fitting for that Creator who brought the human race into existence from the depths of nothingness <note>original: "from the caverns of chaos" or "the cellars of non-existence."</note>. He gave humanity superiority over all other created things. Having decorated human nature with the gift of speech, He clothed it in the robe of knowledge. How can a fragile human being express the proper amount of gratitude?

<summary>The preface begins with a traditional prayer of praise, thanking the Creator for the diversity of the physical world and the unique intellectual gifts granted to humanity.</summary>
<keywords>doxology, theology, creation, human nature, philosophy, Necessary Being, gift of speech</keywords>

...for His mercies? And what power is there in my fragile pen <note>original: "crazy reed"; a reed pen was the standard writing implement, and "crazy" here means frail, cracked, or weak.</note> to lead me successfully through such an ambitious task?

### VERSES.

Unlimited blessings are due to the Prince of the Apostles, the last of the prophets, Muhammad Mustafa <note>Mustafa is a title for Muhammad meaning "the Chosen One."</note>, who rescued those who had gone astray from the wilderness of error and guided them to the right path. It was through him that we achieved—according to the sacred text, “You are the best of nations” <note>A reference to the Quran, Surah Al-Imran 3:110: "You are the best nation produced [as an example] for mankind..."</note>—superiority over all other peoples.

->Muhammad, the lord of all created things;<-

<note>¹ *Possible being* <note>original: *Mumkin* or *Imkānu-l-wujūd*</note> is the exact opposite of *Necessary Being* <note>original: *Wājibu-l-wujūd*</note>. It signifies something whose existence is <term>contingent</term>—meaning it depends on a cause outside itself. It is equivalent to "created" <note>original: *makhlūq*; the OCR likely misread the 'q' as an 'h'.</note>.</note>

<header>PREFACE.</header>
<sig>b</sig>

And may blessings and peace be upon his family and companions, through whose efforts the true faith was consolidated, and who served as our guides on the path of salvation.

Now, this humble and sinful servant, Ikram Ali, proceeds to explain the origin of this work as follows: Following a gracious suggestion from that illustrious gentleman of high dignity and power—the greatest philosopher of the age, an "eleventh intelligence"¹ in wisdom, the generous lord, Mr. Abraham Lockett (may his good fortune continue)—and in compliance with the request of my honored brother and instructor, Maulavi Turab Ali (may his protection <note>original: "shadow"; a common metaphorical idiom in Persian and Urdu for protection or patronage</note> last long), I came to Calcutta. Under the guidance of good fortune, after obtaining the honor of an interview and being received with kind attention and favor, I found that the aforementioned gentleman, because he was disposed to—

***

¹ The word <term>'akl</term> <note>original: 'aql; meaning "intellect" or "reason"</note> is used here in the sense of a *simple essence* or *pure intelligence*. Certain Muslim <note>original: "Mohammedan"</note> scholars hold the following theory on creation: God, they say, created a single intelligence possessed of a twofold existence—one *necessary* and the other *contingent*. This intelligence created a second intelligence like itself and the highest of the nine heavens; that is, the <term>'arsh</term> <note>the Empyrean or the Throne of God</note>. This second intelligence created a third and the second of the nine heavens, and so on, until the tenth intelligence—the last that was created—and the ninth heaven (by which these scholars mean the <term>farsh</term> <note>the earth or ground</note>) were created. Ikram Ali—may his reputation remain untarnished <note>original: "may his shade never blush"</note>—adds an eleventh to the number of intelligences in the person of Mr. Abraham Lockett!

took me entirely under his fostering protection, had me enrolled among the employees <note>original: "servants"; in this context, it refers to officials or civil servants of the company</note> of the Honorable East India Company, and appointed me to a position working under him.

After a few days, with the agreement of His Honor—the noble and most learned man of the age, the foremost among the wise, and the highly powerful Captain John William Taylor, Professor of Urdu (may his good fortune continue!)—he requested that I translate the *Ikhwānu-ṣ-ṣafā* <note>The "Brethren of Purity," a 10th-century secret society in Basra that produced an encyclopedia of philosophy and science; this specific section is a famous fable about animals suing humans in the court of the King of the Jinn</note> into Urdu. 

"But," he said, "write it in very plain and simple language, so that there are no obscure words in it. Moreover, leave out the technical scientific terms and the religious debates, as these are quite difficult.¹ Only the core substance of the matter at hand is required." 

In accordance with this request, I have translated only the essential heart <note>original: "pith"</note> of the subject matter into the Urdu language. I have removed the religious debates and many of the scientific phrases that were not relevant to the conflict between man and the animals. However, I have kept a few discussions and some geometrical and

¹ The word <term>takalluf</term> here literally means "being labored" or "artificial," but "difficulty" conveys the meaning well in this context.

<page-num>xi</page-num>

# PREFACE.

other terms, which bore directly upon the original subject.

In truth, if one considers the execution and the ornate, polished style of this treatise, it is clear that every discourse is a source <note>original: "mine"</note> of elegance and clarity <note>original: "perspicuity"</note>, and every sentence is a treasury of eloquence. Although ordinary readers <note>original: "the common herd"</note> gain nothing more from the literal wording than the basic story of the dispute, scholars who understand the hidden <note>original: "recondite"</note> meanings find pleasure in exploring many subtle questions and matters of speculative theology by grasping the work's true significance.

The authors of this work were ten men (including Abū Salmān, Abu'l-ḥasan, and Abū Aḥmad) who lived together in Basra,¹ and dedicated their lives to constant research into science and religion; they eventually composed fifty-one treatises. In these volumes, many rare and wonderful sciences are discussed, and this current treatise—concerning the contest between humans and the animals <note>original: "beasts"</note>—is one of them.

In this work, arguments based on theoretical principles <note>original: "à priori"</note> and those based on observed experience <note>original: "à posteriori"</note> are presented by both sides with great skill, and, in

---

¹ Commonly called Basra <note>original: "Bussorah"</note>.

<summary>The author praises the literary and intellectual depth of the "Ikhwānu-ṣ-ṣafā," noting that while it appears to be a simple fable, it contains profound theological and scientific insights written by a group of scholars in Basra.</summary>
<keywords>Ikhwānu-ṣ-ṣafā, Basra, speculative theology, Abū Salmān, Abu'l-ḥasan, Abū Aḥmad, Brethren of Purity, philosophy, animal fable</keywords>

--- Page 18 ---
<meta>This page concludes the author's introductory remarks on the purpose of the book, which features a debate between humans and animals, and provides the date and dedication for this translation.</meta>

<page-num>xii</page-num>
<header>PREFACE.</header>

In the end, after much intense debate, mankind is declared the winner.

Furthermore, their goal in this formal debate <note>original: "disputation"</note> is simply to show the superior qualities of the human race. This is why they write at the end of the work:
> "The qualities by which humanity holds power over the animal kingdom <note>original: "brute creation"</note> are diverse knowledge and an understanding of the Divine nature, which we have described in fifty-one separate volumes. The goal of this specific book <note>original: "treatise"</note> has been to use the voices <note>original: "tongues"</note> of animals to illustrate the reality of knowledge and true perceptions of the Divine Being. We have done this so that those who are careless or unaware might be inspired to seek perfection themselves."

The translation of this work was completed during the administration of that embodiment of high nobility, that model of powerful princes, the *Hātim*¹ <note>a reference to an exceptionally generous figure</note> of this era, the Plato of his time, the leader of leaders, and the hero of heroes: His Excellency the Governor-General, Lord Minto—may his good fortune continue forever! This was in the Hijri year 1225 <note>original: "1225 of the Flight"; the Islamic lunar calendar</note> and the year 1810 of the Christian era.

---

¹ *Hātim* was the leader of an Arab tribe called the *Tāï*. His generosity <note>original: "liberality"</note> became so famous that it is now a proverb.

<summary>The author explains that the fable's purpose is to demonstrate human superiority through divine knowledge and concludes with a formal dedication to Lord Minto, dated 1810.</summary>
<keywords>human superiority, divine nature, Lord Minto, Governor-General, 1225 AH, 1810 AD, Hātim al-Tā'ī, Islamic calendar</keywords>

--- Page 19 ---
<meta>This page begins the main narrative of the book, shifting from the translator's preface to the start of the fable itself, which describes the early history of humans and their conflict with animals.</meta>

# CHAPTER I.

->ON THE FIRST APPEARANCE OF THE HUMAN RACE—THEIR STRUGGLE<-
->WITH THE ANIMAL KINGDOM—THE ARRIVAL OF THE ANIMALS<-
->BEFORE THE KING OF THE JINN, BEWARASB THE WISE, TO<-
->BEG FOR RELIEF—THE SAGE’S SUMMONING OF HUMANITY<-
->BEFORE HIM.<-

The events surrounding the first appearance of the human race <note>original: "sons of Adam"</note> are told as follows: While humans were few in number, they constantly fled out of fear of the animals and hid themselves in caves. Driven by the dread and terror of predatory <note>original: "rapacious"</note> beasts, they sought refuge in hills and mountains. They lacked the confidence for even a small group of three or four people to work together to farm the land and eat what they grew, let alone the skill to weave cloth to cover their bodies. In short, they ate whatever fruits, herbs, and wild plants they could find, and they covered their bodies with the leaves of trees. In winter, they lived in warm regions, and in summer, they stayed in cooler ones.

After this state of affairs had continued for a short time and their population had grown, the fear of animals—both harmful and harmless—that had occupied their minds completely vanished. And

<summary>This chapter introduces the early history of humanity, describing how humans originally lived in fear of animals, hiding in caves and surviving on wild plants before their population grew and their fear subsided.</summary>
<keywords>human origins, animal kingdom, Jinn, Bewarasb the Wise, early civilization, survival</keywords>

--- Page 20 ---
<meta>This page continues the narrative of early human history, moving from the growth of the human population to their domestication and exploitation of the animal kingdom, which leads to the animals' suffering and eventual flight.</meta>

# THE BRETHREN OF PURITY <note>original: IKHWANU-S-SAFA</note>

Consequently, they settled in many forts, cities, towns, and villages, and lived in peace. They gathered agricultural tools, and everyone devoted themselves to their specific work. They also caught animals in traps and began using them for riding, carrying loads, and plowing the land. The shoulders of the elephant, horse, camel, donkey, and many other animals—which used to roam freely¹ in the wilderness and forests, grazing wherever they liked without restriction—became raw from constant work <note>original: "excoriated through incessant labour"</note>,² and their backs were bruised and sore. No matter how much they shrieked or cried out, "his lordship," Man, paid little attention. Many wild animals, fearing capture, fled to distant wildernesses. The birds also left their nests and flew³ away, taking their young with them. Every man imagined that all animals were his slaves. Therefore, they constantly made traps and snares, pursuing the creatures with every possible trick and scheme.

¹ Literally: Like a camel without its nose-string <note>This refers to an animal that is unrestrained and free to go anywhere.</note>.
² Literally: Labor of day and night.
³ The only meanings given for the word *uranchhu* <note>original: uṛānchhū</note> in Shakespear’s Dictionary <note>Referring to John Shakespear’s 1817 "Dictionary, Hindustani and English"</note> are "trick," "device," or "fraud." None of these apply here, but the word is correctly translated by the scholar Duncan Forbes. The word *chhu* <note>original: chhū</note>, added to *uran* <note>original: uṛān, the noun for "flight" derived from the verb uṛnā, "to fly"</note>, adds the idea of instantaneity to the flight. *Chhu* is an onomatopoeic word <note>a word that mimics a sound</note>, sounding like the breath exhaled at the end of spells by Hindu jugglers, or at the end of verses from the Quran <note>original: Korān</note> that Muslims <note>original: Musalmāns</note> recite to ward off the evil influence of spirits <note>original: Jinn</note>. It seems to be the equivalent of the English word "presto," used in magic tricks <note>original: legerdemain</note>.

The mule’s speech proceeded as follows: 

“O King! The scriptures which this man has quoted do not imply that we are their slaves and they are our owners. They only mention the blessings which God (may He be exalted) has granted to humans, as is indicated by a text to the following effect: ‘God (may He be exalted) has made the animals subservient to you, just as He has made the sun, the moon, the winds, and the clouds subservient to you.’¹ 

This does not show that these men are our masters and we are their slaves. Instead, it shows that after God (may He be exalted) created all things in heaven and earth, He made every kind of creature dependent on another so that they might derive mutual benefit and protect one another from harm. 

Therefore, in God’s making us subservient to them, His simple motive was that we should be a source of benefit and protection for them. It was not, as they deceitfully and slanderously claim, so that they could be the lords and we the slaves. Before the creation of man, we and our ancestors lived unmolested on the face of the earth, grazing and wandering wherever we pleased, and each of us was occupied with seeking our own food. # CHAPTER II

### DESCRIBING THE CLOSE ATTENTION THE KING OF THE GENII PAID TO THE DISPUTE BETWEEN HUMANS AND ANIMALS, WITH THE GOAL OF REACHING A DECISION.

When the monarch heard these accounts from the animals, he immediately became absorbed in the settlement of this dispute. He commanded the attendance of the judges, the law officers, and all the chiefs and nobles of the <term>Genii</term> <note>original: Genii; these are the Jinn, supernatural beings in Islamic mythology who, like humans, possess free will and are subject to God's judgment</note>. Forthwith, in obedience to his call, they all presented themselves in the royal court. He then addressed the men, saying: "The animals have clearly and fully laid before us the tale and <term>plaint</term> <note>original: plaint; a formal expression of grief or a legal complaint</note> of your tyranny. What do you have to say now in response to them?"

One of the men thereupon saluted the king and spoke as follows: "O refuge of the world! These are all our slaves, and we are their masters. It is fitting for us, then, to exercise authority over them as befits masters, and to work them as we wish. Those among them who have willingly bowed to our yoke have found favor with God; and those who have rebelled against us have, so to speak, rebelled against God."

-> **THE BRETHREN OF PURITY** <note>original: Ikhwanu-s-Safa; a secret society of Muslim philosophers in 10th-century Iraq who wrote an encyclopedia of philosophy and science</note> <-

The king noted, "A claim without evidence is not allowed in a court of law. Produce some authority and some proof beyond your word."

The human representative replied, "There are many logical and traditional arguments <note>original: "traditional arguments"; this refers to evidence from religious scripture or revelation, contrasted with "rational arguments" based on logic</note> which prove our claim."

The king asked what these arguments were, and the speaker began as follows: "Look at the excellence with which God (may He be exalted) has created us! He has given us suitable limbs, each well-suited to our needs; a symmetrical body; an upright posture; and reason and knowledge, through which we may distinguish between good and bad. Indeed, we can even discover and explain the workings of the entire heavens! In whom, besides ourselves, are these excellent qualities found? Therefore, it is clear that we are the masters, and these animals are the slaves."

The king then said to the animals, "Now, how do you respond to this?"

They replied, "These arguments do not prove their claim."

# CHAPTER 3

### ON THE DIFFERENCE OF FORM AND FIGURE IN HUMANS AND ANIMALS

"**THIS** difference is explained in this way: When God created humans, they were completely naked—there was absolutely nothing on their bodies to protect them from heat and cold. They used to eat the fruits of the forest and cover their bodies with the leaves of trees. For this reason, He made their stature upright and tall, so that they might pluck the fruits from the trees and eat without difficulty, and pluck the leaves to put them to use. However, our food is grass, and so He made our bodies bent downward, so that we might feed easily and suffer no hardship."

The king asked: "And what do you say to this declaration of God's: 'We have made man in the most perfect symmetry'?" <note>original: A reference to the *Qur'an* (Al-Koran), Chapter 95 (The Fig), Verse 4: "Verily we created man of a most excellent fabric." The translator George Sale notes that commentators generally interpret this to mean that humans were created with beautiful physical proportions and a great perfection of mind.</note>

The animal replied, saying, "Refuge of the world! <note>term: 'Asylum of the world'—a traditional title of honor for a king, implying he is a place of safety and protection for his subjects</note> In addition to the obvious meaning of our Lord's word, there are many interpretations that only the wise can understand."

<summary>The animal representative argues that physical posture—upright for humans to gather fruit and bent for animals to graze—is a practical divine gift for survival. When the King cites scripture regarding human "symmetry," the animal prepares to offer a deeper philosophical interpretation.</summary>

<keywords>animal anatomy, divine design, Qur'an 95:4, George Sale, human anatomy, religious interpretation, Ikhwanu-s-Safa, physical symmetry</keywords>

# IKHWANU-S-SAFA
<note>original: The "Brethren of Purity," a secret society of Muslim philosophers in 10th-century Basra who wrote an encyclopedia of science and philosophy.</note>

"Let the scholars be asked to explain this passage." And so a learned doctor, following the king’s command, explained the meaning of this text in the following way:

> "The day on which God (may He be exalted) formed Adam was a fortunate one. The stars, each in its high position, were shining brightly and were perfectly aligned for the physical elements to receive their shaped forms. Because of this, the human form appeared beautiful, the posture upright, and the limbs perfect."<sup>1</sup>

However, the words translated as "in the most perfect symmetry" are shown by the following text to be capable of another interpretation:

> "God (may He be exalted) made man with moderate proportions,"<sup>2</sup>

neither very tall nor very short. The king remarked that "such beautiful proportions and the fitness of the body parts were enough to prove human superiority." The animals replied:

> "That is the case for us as well. The Most High God has also given us limbs that are well-proportioned and adapted to our needs. Regarding this specific excellence, we and the humans are equal."

<sup>2</sup> *The Quran* <note>original: Al Korān</note>; Chapter 82 (The Cleaving), verses 7 and 8. "O man! It should be noted that the Urdu translator omits the translation of the final six words of the Arabic text.

The men replied, “Where is the balance of limbs <note>original: proportionality of the members</note> in your case? Yours are the most hideous <note>original: forbidding</note> forms, with uneven heights and awkward, ill-shaped legs. For among you is the camel, which is very tall with a long neck and a short tail; and the elephant, with a body of immense height, bulk, and weight, two long tusks projecting beyond the mouth, large broad ears, and tiny little eyes. There are also the ox and the buffalo, with long tails, thick horns, and no teeth in the upper jaw; and the fat-tailed ram, with heavy horns and fat hindquarters <note>original: buttocks</note>. There is the male goat, which has a long beard and lacks hindquarters; and the hare, which is very short but has long ears. Similarly, there are many meat-eating <note>original: carnivorous</note> and plant-eating <note>original: herbivorous</note> beasts, and many birds, whose height and build are out of proportion, where one body part bears no relation to the others.”

<margin>The humans did not understand</margin>

# IKHWANU-S-SAFA
<note>original: "Brethren of Purity," a 10th-century secret society of Muslim philosophers who wrote an encyclopedia covering science, religion, and ethics.</note>

“...created everything for some special reason? Only the learned and He Himself can understand His secret purpose.”

At this, the same man remarked, “If you are the wise one <note>original: sage</note> of the animals, perhaps you will tell us why the camel’s neck was made so long.”

The animal replied, saying: “The reason is that his legs are long. If his neck were short, it would be difficult for him to graze; so his neck has been formed long so that he may feed easily <note>original: with advantage</note>. Furthermore, using the strength of that same neck, he can stand up from the ground and reach any part of his body with his lips to scratch himself.

In the same way, the elephant’s trunk is long to serve the purpose of a neck. His ears are large to enable him to drive away flies and mosquitoes, preventing them from entering his eyes and mouth; for his mouth remains constantly open because of his tusks. His tusks are long so that he may defend himself from predatory <note>original: rapacious</note> beasts. 

Likewise, the hare’s ears are made long so that, having a delicate body and thin skin, she may cover herself with them in winter and spread them out like a bed in summer. In short, God (may He be exalted) has granted every animal the limbs He saw fit. Regarding this point, speaking through the prophet Moses, He declares: ‘It was our Lord who gave everything its form, and then guided it correctly.’ <note>original: Al Korān; chapter 20, verse 52</note> The meaning of this is that He gave to every creature the shape and tools it needed to survive.”

<summary>The animal representative explains how the physical features of the camel, elephant, and hare are purposefully designed by God to help them survive and thrive. This serves as a theological argument for the "divine design" of nature as found in the Quran.</summary>

# IKHWANU-S-SAFA
<note>original: "Brethren of Purity," a 10th-century secret society of Muslim philosophers who wrote an encyclopedia covering science, religion, and ethics.</note>

...gave to each creature the form which He saw best adapted to it, and then guided it onto the right path. As for what you consider beauty—which you pride yourselves upon and which you, in your arrogance, believe proves you are our masters <note>original: lords</note> and we are your slaves—you are mistaken.

Beauty within each species is simply that which is admired among its own kind, and because of this, its members are drawn to one another. This is the reason for the reproduction <note>original: propagation</note> of the species. A beautiful form in one species is not admired by another. Every animal sets his heart on the female of his own kind and does not desire <note>original: covet</note> the female of another, even if she is more beautiful. 

Humans, too, desire their own kind. Those who are Black do not prefer those who are white, and those who are white do not set their affections on those who are Black. <note>This passage reflects historical views on race and beauty found in the 19th-century translation. The original translator noted that a further passage was omitted here because it was "more suited to Oriental than European taste."</note> Therefore, your physical beauty is not a result of your status as masters, and you should not consider yourselves superior to us because of it.”

# CHAPTER IV.

## REGARDING THE VARIOUS COMPLAINTS <note>original: plaints; a formal legal accusation or expression of grief</note> BROUGHT AGAINST THE MEN BY EACH ANIMAL.

The King turned to the men and said, "You have heard this animal's answer; now state whatever else you have to say." They said, "We still have many arguments in reserve that establish our right of ownership <note>original: title</note>. These are some of them: we buy and sell them; feed and clothe them; shelter them from heat and cold; overlook <note>original: shut our eyes to</note> their faults; protect them from being harmed by predators <note>original: beasts of prey</note>; and provide them with medicine <note>original: physic them</note> out of kindness when they are sick. These dealings of ours with them are the result of compassion and tenderness. It is the practice of all masters to deal tenderly and compassionately with their slaves under all circumstances."

Upon hearing this, the King said to the animals, "Give your reply to this." One of them spoke, saying: "As for this man's assertion that 'we buy and sell the animals,' this custom exists among humans as well. For example, when the Persians <note>A reference to the Sassanid Empire or the broader Persian culture, which the 10th-century authors of this text used as a standard historical example.</note> conquer the—

King, the donkeys <note>original: asses</note> said: "When we fall into their grasp, they load our backs with bricks, stones, iron, wood, and many other heavy burdens. After a scholar debates whether the pig is a herbivore or carnivore, the pig itself begins to speak, detailing the various cultural and religious attitudes—ranging from extreme revulsion to medicinal appreciation—that humans hold toward its species.</meta>

<page-num>28</page-num>

# IKHWANU-S-SAFA <note>original: "The Brethren of Purity"</note>

"...the giraffe <note>original: "cameleopard"</note> is a composite creature made of the ox, the camel, and the leopard, much like the ostrich, whose form resembles both a bird and a camel."

The pig replied to the camel, saying, "I know nothing. What shall I say, and about whom shall I complain? Many diverse and conflicting opinions are held concerning us. Those who are Muslims <note>original: "Musalmāns"</note> look upon us as transformed <note>original: "metamorphosed"</note> and accursed. They regard our forms as loathsome and our meat as unclean, and they avoid even mentioning us. On the other hand, the Greeks eat our meat with relish, consider it a blessing from God, and believe that offering us as a sacrifice is a righteous act. 

The Jews, conversely, feel hatred and hostility toward us. They abuse and curse us even though we have done nothing wrong, simply because they are hostile toward the Christians and the Greeks. Meanwhile, the Armenians place us on equal footing with the ox and the goat; and because of our fatness, the plumpness of our meat, and our great fertility <term>fecundity</term>, they regard us as even better than those animals. 

Greek physicians commonly use our fat as medicine and indeed keep it in stock along with their other remedies. Herdsmen and grooms keep us near their cattle and horses in stables and pastures; for they believe that by our presence among them, the cattle and horses are protected from—"

...many disasters. Enchanters and magicians place our skins in their books and among their mystical diagrams. Shoemakers and workers in leather pluck and preserve the bristles of our manes and whiskers with great eagerness, for they are of great use to them. We are perplexed and can say nothing. Whom shall we praise, and whom shall we blame?”

When the pig had finished speaking, the ass looked toward the hare, who was standing near<sup>1</sup> the camel, and said to her, “Tell the king all the wrongs that man has inflicted on your kind. Perhaps <note>original: "peradventure"</note> the king may become gracious and set our captive brothers <note>original: "brethren"</note> free from their clutches.” 

The hare replied, saying: “We dwell at a distance from them. We abandoned their territory and took up our home in valleys <note>original: "vales"</note> and forests, and therefore we are safe from their tyranny. But we are quite at a loss to understand the behavior of the dogs and predatory animals <note>original: "animals of prey"</note>; for they help the men to catch us and guide them to our hiding places <note>original: "haunts"</note>. The deer, the ox, the camel, the goat, the wild animals, and all those among our kin who have sought shelter in the mountains—they deliver them as captives into the hands of men.” 

The hare continued: “The hounds are to be excused for doing this; their helping them is as it should be, for they also have a taste for—”

## CHAPTER V.

->IN PRAISE OF THE HORSE<- <note>original: "Eulogy"</note>

When the men heard the hare’s words, one of them said, “Enough! Hold your peace. You have defamed the horse quite enough! If you had been aware of his superiority to all other animals and his submissiveness to man, you would not have spoken such nonsense.” The king asked the man, “In what way is he superior?” He replied, saying, “Sire! He possesses many excellent qualities and virtues. His form is handsome; each of his limbs is well-proportioned; his figure and build are pleasant to behold; his senses are sharp; his color is pure; he excels in wisdom <note>original: "sagacity"</note>; he is fast in running; he is submissive to his rider, moving instantly to the right or left, forward or backward, exactly as he is commanded. He does not flinch from hard work; he is possessed of such good manners that as long as the rider is on his back, he will not defecate or urinate <note>original: "dung or stale"</note>; and should his tail somehow become wet with mud <note>original: "puddle"</note> or water, he will not shake it, lest the rider should be splashed <note>original: "bespattered"</note>. He has the strength of an elephant;”

<summary>A human defender rebuts the hare's insults by listing the horse's many virtues, including its physical beauty, intelligence, obedience, and refined manners toward its rider.</summary>
<keywords>horse, animal trial, horse virtues, submissiveness, sagacity, animal behavior, human-animal relationship</keywords>

--- Page 50 ---
<meta>This page continues the human's defense of the horse's virtues, specifically focusing on its strength, bravery, and agility, before the hare offers a sharp rebuttal regarding the horse's lack of discernment.</meta>

<page-num>32</page-num>

<header>IKHWANU-S-SAFA <note>original: "The Brethren of Purity," a 10th-century secret society of philosophers in Basra who wrote an encyclopedia; this section is from their "Fable of the Animals."</note></header>

He gallops away carrying the weight of his rider in armor and helmet, along with his own bridle, saddle, and iron armor, weighing a total of five hundred *mans* <note>original: "mans"; a South Asian unit of weight (maund) that varied by region, here used to emphasize a massive load</note>. He is so patient and humble <note>original: "meek"</note> that even though he receives sword cuts on his chest in battle and spear thrusts in his heart, he still utters no complaint. He possesses such swiftness <note>original: "fleetness"</note> that the wind cannot keep up with the dust raised by his hooves. In majesty <note>original: "stateliness"</note>, he is like a strong, mature bull <note>The original English translator notes: Here the Urdu translator has either made a mistake or taken liberties with the original text. The Arabic version says: "His walk is like the walk of the bull in its majesty; his amble is like the amble of the fox; his charge is like the rush of a huge mass of rock when a torrent rolls it downward; and his leap is like the leap of the leopard; and he competes in a race for a prize against any who seek victory."</note>. In agility <note>original: "springiness"</note>, he resembles a leopard.

If his rider has placed a bet on him, he flies with speed and brings his rider across the finish line first. In what animal, except the horse, can such excellent qualities be found?

The hare remarked that "these excellent qualities are accompanied by a great flaw <note>original: "defect"</note> which overshadows <note>original: "obscures"</note> them all." The king <note>original: "monarch"</note> asked, "What is this flaw?" The hare replied, "He is very stupid and ignorant. He is completely unable to distinguish between a friend and an enemy <note>original: "foe"</note>. If an enemy is on his back, he submits to him; and when urged on by that enemy in a fight, he rushes to attack the very person in whose house he was born <note>original: "foaled"</note> and raised. In possessing this trait <note>original: "property"</note>, he resembles a sword—"

<header>IKHWANU-S-SAFA <note>original: "The Brethren of Purity."</note></header>

...but then this is a lifeless object and cannot distinguish <note>original: "discriminate"</note> between friend and foe. If it happens to fall on the neck of its owner or maker, it severs the head from the body just as unhesitatingly as it would a foe's. It perceives no difference between its own master and a stranger.

This quality is found in humans too; for they harbor hostility <note>original: "enmity"</note> toward their parents, their brothers, sisters, and other relations, and practice all kinds of deceit! Conduct that is appropriate only toward an enemy, they practice toward their own kin. In childhood, they receive nourishment from their mothers' breasts and are fondled in their parents' laps, yet in adulthood, they become their enemies. In the same way, they drink the milk of animals, make garments from their skins and hair, and derive benefit from them; and then, after all that, they slaughter and skin those very animals, disembowel <note>original: "embowel"</note> them, and make them taste the fire! Inhumanity and hardheartedness drive from their minds the memory of the obligations and benefits for which they are in debt to them.

When the hare had finished her criticisms <note>original: "animadversions"</note> of humans and the horse, the donkey <note>original: "ass"</note> said to her:

"Enough! Too much criticism <note>original: "censure"</note> is not appropriate. Where is there a being upon whom God (may He be exalted) has bestowed many excellent qualities and good gifts, but has not also withheld one blessing that outweighs all those excellent qualities? And who is..."

<meta>This page continues the donkey’s response to the hare, arguing that no created being—whether celestial, human, or supernatural—is perfect, as perfection belongs only to God.</meta>

<page-num>34</page-num>

<header>IKHWANU-S-SAFA <note>original: "The Brethren of Purity"</note></header>

...he from whom God has withheld all favors, and has not bestowed on him at least one blessing that has not been given to anyone else? There is no one in the world who possesses every noble quality and every blessing. The favors of that Being <note>God</note> who grants even when not asked are not limited to any one thing, but are bestowed freely on all—on some in greater, and on some in less measure. On those to whom He has granted the honor of leadership <note>original: "dignity of rule"</note>, He has also placed the mark of service <note>original: "servitude"</note>. 

Look at the high status He has given to the sun and moon! He has granted them light, visibility, grandeur, and superior excellence to such an extent that some nations, in their ignorance, worship them as their gods. Yet He has not spared them from the flaw of eclipses, so that the wise might see that if these were truly gods, they would never be covered in darkness and would never fade away <note>original: "wane"</note>. 

In the same way, He has given light and brilliance to all the stars, but with the flaw that they vanish in the sun's glare. They remain in constant motion <note>original: "perpetual revolution"</note> so that the signs of their being created things may be clearly seen. The same applies to the Jinn <note>original: "Genii"</note>, to humanity, and to the angels. If anyone possesses many noble qualities, a flaw or two is certain to accompany them. Perfection belongs to the Most High God alone, and to no one else."

### IKHWANU-S-SAFA <note>The Brethren of Purity</note>

When the donkey <note>original: "ass"</note> had finished speaking, the ox said:

"It is the duty of those whom God (may He be exalted) has blessed so generously with gifts that He has withheld from others, to show their gratitude—that is, they should allow those others to share in those generous kindnesses. Just as God has given the sun its light, and it sheds that light generously over the entire universe without expecting anything in return <note>original: "lays no obligations on any thing"</note>, so too the moon and stars, each according to its degree, shed their rays on the world and place no one in their debt. In the same way, it is the responsibility of humans to share freely with the animals the generous favors that God (may He be exalted) has bestowed upon them."

When the ox had said this, all the animals wept aloud and cried, "O just king, have pity on us, and deliver us from the tyranny of these unjust men!"

Hearing this, the king turned to all the sages and learned scholars of the Jinn <note>original: "Genii"</note> who were present and said, "Have you heard the accounts of human injustice, pitilessness, and oppression that the animals have shared?"

They answered, saying: "We have heard, and it is all true. Indeed, we witness it day and night. Their wrongdoing is obvious <note>original: "manifest"</note> to every wise and intelligent being. It was for this reason that the Jinn, too, abandoned their lands and fled to the forests and wilderness, seeking refuge in the hills and mountains. Because of their..."

# IKHWANU-S-SAFA <note>The Brethren of Purity</note>

...evil deeds and wicked ways, they (that is, the Jinn) stopped going to inhabited places, and yet they still cannot escape from human wickedness. To such a degree do humans think ill of and distrust us, that if a child, a woman, or any ignorant, foolish man falls ill, they invariably say, "He is Jinn-stricken," or "He is Jinn-possessed." They continually harbor evil thoughts of us in their minds and pray to be preserved from the wickedness of the Jinn—despite the fact that no one has ever witnessed a single case of a Jinn killing or wounding a man, or snatching away his clothes, or robbing, or committing burglary in any of their houses, or picking their pockets, or cutting their sleeves <note>original: "rending their sleeves"; see note below</note>, or breaking open the lock of any shop, or beating any traveler, or inciting <note>original: "fomenting"</note> rebellion against any of their kings, or plundering anyone, or imprisoning anyone! On the contrary, these are human habits: they are always intent on injuring one another; and yet they never repent of such deeds or guard against them."

When this Jinn had also finished speaking, the mace-bearer <note>A ceremonial official who carries a staff of office</note> called out aloud and said: "Sirs! It is now evening; the Court is closed. Depart to your various homes and attend again in the morning."

***

<note>It is the custom in Arabia (and, I believe, in Persia) for people to carry money and other items in the sleeves of their vests instead of in pockets. The crime mentioned here refers to tearing or cutting the sleeve to steal the money kept inside.</note>

## CHAPTER VI.

### THE KING CONSULTS WITH HIS MINISTER

When the king had withdrawn from the court <note>original: "tribunal"</note>, he spoke to his chief minister, <term>Bīdār</term> <note>A name meaning "wakeful" or "vigilant" in Persian.</note>, in <insert>private</insert> <note>The words 'private', 'consult', and 'enter' were underlined in the original manuscript by a later reader.</note> with these words: “You have heard the discussion between the humans and the animals. Now, what do you advise? How should this case be decided? In your opinion, what is the best course to pursue?”

The minister was a very wise and shrewd man. After bowing deeply <note>original: "making his obeisance"</note>, saluting the king, and invoking blessings on him, he spoke as follows: “In my opinion, it would be better for your Majesty to summon all the judges, law officers, and scholars of the <term>Jinn</term> <note>original: "Genii"; spirits in Islamic mythology who, like humans, possess free will and are subject to judgment.</note> to your presence. You should <insert>consult</insert> them on this case, for this is no small matter <note>original: "no trifling dispute"</note>. It is not clear which side justice favors. In such a case, consultation is necessary. By seeking the counsel of many, a matter is cleared of confusion <note>original: "divested of obscurity"</note>. It is the duty of the wise and far-seeing not to <insert>enter</insert> upon such complex matters without advice and consultation.”

Following this advice, the king commanded, saying: “So be it!”

***

¹ “Where there is no counsel, the people fall; but in the multitude of counselors there is safety.” —Proverbs 11:14 <note>The original text cited Proverbs 24:6; both verses emphasize the wisdom of seeking multiple opinions.</note>

<summary>Following the first day of the trial, the King of the Jinn seeks the advice of his minister Bīdār, who recommends convening a larger council of experts to help resolve the difficult dispute between humans and animals.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the narrative following the King of the Jinn's decision to consult with his advisors. Having accepted his minister's suggestion, the King now summons the various noble and learned clans of his realm to hear the grievances of the animals against humanity.</meta>

# IKHWANU-S-SAFA <note>original: "The Brethren of Purity"; a 10th-century secret society in Basra that produced an encyclopedia of philosophy and science, including this animal fable.</note>

“Let all the chiefs and nobles of the <term>Jinn</term> <note>original: "Genii"; supernatural beings in Islamic mythology created from smokeless fire.</note> be present.” And so, as commanded, the judges of the race of <term>Birjīs</term> <note>Associated with the planet Jupiter, representing justice and law.</note>, the law officers of the tribe of <term>Nāhid</term> <note>Associated with the planet Venus.</note>, the wise descendants of Bidā <note>Likely referring to the planet Mercury.</note>, the sages of the family of Lukmān <note>A legendary wise man in Islamic tradition, often compared to Aesop.</note>, the experienced of the race of Hāmān, the wise of the family of <term>Kaiwān</term> <note>Associated with the planet Saturn.</note>, and the enterprising and resolute of the house of <term>Bahrām</term> <note>Associated with the planet Mars.</note> were all in attendance.

The King addressed them as follows: “These humans and animals have come before us with mutual grievances and have sought refuge in our kingdom. All the animals complain of humanity's injustice and oppression. Advise me on the course to be followed with them and how their case should be resolved.”

One of the scholars of the race of Nāhid who was present said: “In my opinion, it is right that all the animals submit a written petition of their case and the wrongs they have endured at the hands of humans. Then, the scholars should be called upon to issue a legal ruling on this matter. If any plan of escape for them is decided upon, the judges and law officers can decree accordingly—whether it be that the humans should sell them, grant them their liberty, or ease their misery and treat them with kindness. Should the humans ignore the decree of the judges and the animals flee because of their oppression, then the animals should be held entirely innocent of any wrongdoing or offense.”

<meta>This page continues the debate within the court of the King of the Jinn regarding the legal and practical solutions to the animals' complaints against human tyranny. # IKHWANU-S-SAFA <note>original: "The Brethren of Purity"; an influential 10th-century secret society in Basra, known for their philosophical encyclopedia.</note>

The monarch, on hearing this, asked: "What do you all say to this?" All pronounced it "excellent, and exactly the right advice for the occasion." Everyone agreed, except for one of the <term>enterprisers</term> <note>Referring to the resolute and action-oriented advisors from the house of Bahrām mentioned on the previous page.</note>, who did not approve of this plan. He observed, "If these men consent to sell the animals, who will pay the cost?" The same law officer replied, "The King." He responded, saying, "From where will the King obtain such a large sum of money?" The law officer replied, "It will be paid from the treasury." The enterpriser replied again, saying: "There is not enough money in the treasury to cover the cost. Besides, some of the men will not sell; they have great need of them and are completely indifferent to selling them, even for their full value. For example, kings, <term>wazīrs</term> <note>original: "wazīrs"; high-ranking government ministers or advisors in Islamic states.</note>, and many wealthy people who cannot travel without a <term>conveyance</term> <note>A vehicle or means of transport, such as a horse-drawn carriage or mount.</note> of some kind would never consent to part with them and would refuse to carry out the decree."

The monarch asked, "What, then, in your opinion, is the better course?" He replied, "In my opinion, it is advisable that the King order the animals to conspire together and flee from their captivity in a single night. Let them depart far from human territory, just as the antelope, the <term>hog-deer</term> <note>A small species of deer native to South and Southeast Asia, named for its habit of running through the underbrush with its head down like a hog.</note>, and many other wild and savage beasts have already left their lands and fled. When the men wake in the morning and find them gone, how will they carry their burdens? And what will they ride?"

<meta>This page continues the discussion in the court of the King of the Jinn. Following the suggestion that the animals should flee, the debate turns to the logistical impossibility of an escape and the supernatural intervention required to overcome human security.</meta>

# IKHWANU-S-SAFA <note>original: "The Brethren of Purity"; a secret society of Muslim philosophers in 10th-century Basra.</note>

"Having no other options and because of the long distances they would have to travel, the humans would be unable to pursue them and would quietly accept their loss. In this way, the animals' escape would be successful."

The King approved of this plan and asked everyone present if they also agreed with what had been said. A wise man who was a descendant of Lukmān <note>A legendary sage in Islamic tradition, often associated with fables and moral wisdom.</note> replied, saying: "This is not wise advice. Such a plan goes against reason and is completely impossible because most of the animals are locked up at night in human custody. The stable doors are barred, and watchmen are stationed over them. How, then, could they all escape?"

The <term>enterpriser</term> <note>original: "enterpriser"; here meaning a bold or resourceful advisor.</note> replied, saying: "The King could order all the <term>genii</term> <note>original: "genii"; Jinn, supernatural spirits in Islamic mythology capable of influencing the physical world.</note> to go there on the chosen night to unlock the stable doors, untie the ropes from the animals' legs, and set them free. They could also restrain the watchmen and not release them until the animals had escaped far from human territory. This would earn the King a very great reward. I have offered this advice to Your Majesty, motivated by pity for the animals' condition. If Your Majesty, in your goodness, decides on this act of kindness, God (may He be exalted) will aid Your Majesty as well. True gratitude for God's mercies consists of this: helping and freeing the oppressed. It is said to be recorded in the scriptures of some of the prophets—"

--- Page 59 ---
<meta>This page continues the debate in the court of the King of the Jinn regarding a plan to use supernatural powers to help animals escape from human control. It focuses on the responsibilities of a ruler and the potential consequences of Jinn intervention in human affairs.</meta>

# IKHWANU-S-SAFA <note>original: "The Brethren of Purity"; a 10th-century secret society of philosophers in Basra who wrote an encyclopedia of sciences and fables.</note>

"...that the Most High God has spoken, saying:
> 'O King! I have not granted you dominion on the earth so that you may accumulate riches and preoccupy yourself with the greed and desires of the world, but so that you may administer justice to the oppressed. For I, too, grant justice to them, even if they are unbelievers.'"

The King again asked everyone present, "What do you say to this?" All approved of it and said, "That is the proper course"—everyone, that is, except a wise man of the tribe of Kaiwān <note>original: "Kaiwān"; the Persian name for the planet Saturn, traditionally associated with wisdom, old age, and deep thought.</note>. He was not satisfied with the plan. After invoking blessings on the King and bowing respectfully, he began as follows:

"This is a task of enormous difficulty; it cannot be achieved in any way. It involves many evils and dangers that could never be repaired."

The monarch asked, "What are the evils you fear in this plan? Explain them, so that we may understand them as well."

He explained, saying, "Sire! He who proposed this method of freeing the animals made a great mistake. As soon as the humans rose in the morning and missed the animals, and became certain of their flight, they would be sure that this was the work of no human being. They would know that it could not possibly have been planned and carried out by the animals themselves, but that this was the machination and treachery of the Jinn <note>original: "Genii"; supernatural spirits in Islamic theology who, like humans, have free will and live in a parallel society.</note>."

The King replied, saying: "True, undoubtedly true; they will suspect us."

What is there, therefore, to be afraid of?"

## CHAPTER VII.

### THE CONFLICT BETWEEN MANKIND AND THE JINN <note>original: "Genii"</note>.

The sage, obeying the King’s command, told the story of this conflict as follows: “In ancient times, before God had created Adam, the Jinn <note>original: "Genii"; supernatural spirits in Islamic theology often depicted as living alongside humans in a parallel dimension.</note> occupied the entire surface of the earth, the forests, and all inhabited lands; the seas and rivers were also under their control. When a long time had passed in this way, and prophets had risen among them, and they had received laws regarding religion and the state, along with many other blessings, they fell into disobedience and sin. They ignored the instructions and warnings of the prophets and filled the entire earth with wickedness. Because of their violence and wrongdoing, the earth and all its inhabitants were driven to cry out and lament, pleading like humble subjects before the throne of God. When another age passed and their hypocrisy and violence continued to increase, God (may He be praised <note>original: "may He be exalted"</note>) sent an army of angels to the earth, who came and punished the Jinn and expelled them from

How are we to decide between all these who have come here to complain, and who have sought refuge in our territories? How are we to judge and dismiss them from our kingdom with satisfaction?"

The sage replied, saying: "Good advice is the result of careful thought. Nothing can be accomplished in haste. At present, I think it is best for your Majesty to preside tomorrow morning in the judgment hall, have all of them (meaning both the humans and the animals) brought before you, and hear the evidence and arguments of both. In the end, God will set them free, just as He set free the children of Israel from the tyranny of Pharaoh; and as He delivered the descendants of David from the oppression of Nebuchadnezzar, and the tribe of Himyar from the violence of the house of Tubba;¹ and as He delivered the races of Sasan² and Adnan from the oppression of the Greeks and the race of Ardashir³ respectively. ## CHAPTER VIII.

### ON THE MEN TAKING COUNSEL TOGETHER.

At this time, the King was in his council chamber, consulting with his minister, his chiefs, and his nobles. Elsewhere, the men—seventy in number and inhabitants of various cities—were gathered in their own residence, consulting one another. Each person spoke whatever thoughts came to mind <note>original: "each spoke conjecturally" or "each conjectured," as indicated in the Arabic text</note>. One of them spoke as follows: "You all heard the words and discussions that took place today between us and our slaves <note>In this allegory, the "slaves" are the animals, whom the humans claim to own by right.</note>, and yet, up to this moment, the dispute remains unsettled. Does anyone have any idea what the King has decided regarding us?" 

All answered, saying, "We do not know; however, we believe that the King is perplexed <note>original: "posed"</note> by this very matter. It is quite possible that he will not appear tomorrow." 

Another said, "I think he will consult his minister in private tomorrow regarding our case." 

Another said, "He will assemble the doctors and the learned men tomorrow, and—"

If we were to carry out such a decree <term>legal ruling</term>, we would be ruined! Do not even mention this!” Those who had been willing to sell asked, “What harm is there in this?” 

They replied, “If we sell the animals, we shall suffer great inconvenience and distress. We shall lose all the advantages of having milk to drink, meat to eat, and skins and hair to make clothes, as well as the other uses to which we apply them. Death is preferable to such a life! The same distress would also fall upon the residents of cities and towns. They, too, have a great need for animals. Under no circumstances decide on selling them or setting them free; indeed, do not even think of such a thing! If they would be content with a lightening <term>alleviation</term> of their burdens and kind treatment, that would be all well and good.”

## CHAPTER IX.

### ON THE CONSULTATION HELD BY THE ANIMALS.

When the king left the judgment hall <note>original: rose up from the judgment-seat</note> and everyone was dismissed to return to their homes, the animals also gathered and held a meeting. One of them said: "You heard all the arguments that took place today between us and our opponents, and the matter at hand is still not settled. Now, in your opinion, what is the proper thing to do?"

Another replied, saying: "We will go and weep before the King tomorrow morning and protest against their oppression. Perhaps the King will have pity on us and release us from slavery <term>bondage</term>. As it was, he showed us some degree of kindness today. However, it is not right for kings and judges <note>The original Arabic text adds "and judges" here.</note> to pass judgment without hearing evidence and arguments. But evidence and arguments are made stronger by eloquent speaking and fluency of speech; as the prophet has said..."

<sup>1</sup> *Ḥasharāt* <note>winged insects</note> and *hawāmm* <note>creeping reptiles</note> are synonymous words, meaning, according to the dictionaries <note>original: "Lexicons"</note>, *all small animals that creep upon the earth*. However, a learned scholar <term>maulavi</term> assured me that the terms are applied to *animals that creep upon the earth, or insects that fly in the air and live no longer than a year*. Yet the author of the *Brethren of Purity* <note>original: "Ikhwānu-ṣ-ṣafā"</note> evidently uses the first of the two words to mean *winged insects that live for only a year*; and the other to mean *reptiles, or creeping things*. The Urdu translator therefore makes a mistake <note>original: "blunders"</note> when he mentions the *earth-worm* <note>original: "kechwā"</note> and the *red velvet insect* <note>original: "bīr bahūṭī"</note> as examples of *ḥasharāt* <note>insects</note>. No examples are provided in the original text.

# CHAPTER 10.

### AN ACCOUNT OF THE FIRST MESSENGER'S JOURNEY.

WHEN the first messenger went to the king <note>original: "monarch"</note> of the meat-eating <note>original: "carnivorous"</note> animals, Abu-'l-Harith <note>original: "Abu-'l hāriṣ"</note>—the lion—he said: "A dispute is taking place between humans <note>original: "men"</note> and the animals before the King of the Jinn <note>original: "Genii"</note>. The animals have sent messengers to all the various tribes of animals so that they might come and help them; they have sent me to attend to your Majesty. Please order one of the leaders of your armies to return with me, so that he may go there, take part with his brothers, and argue against the humans when his turn comes."

He replied, saying: "They declare that all the animals are their slaves, and that they are our masters <note>original: "their lords"</note>."

king; for the king is like the head, and his subjects are like the limbs of the body. As long as both the king and his subjects continue to fulfill their assigned duties,¹ affairs proceed correctly, and order and good government prevail in the State.”

“...courage, and rivalry <gloss>1</gloss> will be of use there, I am the one for such a task; grant me permission to depart, so that I may go there and accomplish this business thoroughly.” The king replied, saying: “Not one of these qualities is needed there.”

The panther said: “If there is a need there for leaping, bounding, seizing, and holding firmly, I take it upon myself to accomplish the task.”

The wolf said: “If there is an occasion there to attack, plunder, and loot <note>original: "spoil"</note>, I will accomplish it.”

The fox said: “If cunning is required there, I am the one for it.”

The mongoose said: “If searching, stealing, and hiding are needed there, I will undertake to manage it.”

The monkey said: “If dancing, skipping about, and mimicking are required there, I am the one for it.”

The cat said: “If fawning <note>Seeking favor through exaggerated affection</note>, cultivating friendship, and begging are needed there, I will accomplish the business.”

The dog said: “If watching, barking, and tail-wagging are required there, I am the one for it.”

The rat said: “If setting things on fire, and mischief of any kind are needed there, I am the one for the business.”

# CHAPTER XI.

## ON THE QUALITIES OF AN EMISSARY.

The leopard asked the monarch, “What are those qualities which an emissary <note>original: "emissary"—an official messenger or representative sent on a mission</note> should possess? Please explain them.” The monarch replied, “An emissary should possess intelligence and an eloquent and persuasive way of speaking. He should not forget anything he hears but should carefully store it in his memory. He should reveal his secret thoughts to no one. He should faithfully perform what his duty demands and should strictly fulfill his promises. He should not be excessively talkative <note>original: "loquacious"—tending to talk a great deal.</note>. In any matter, he should not add anything of his own; instead, he should say exactly what he was commanded to say. He should work zealously on matters that will benefit the person who sends him.¹ If the opposing party

¹ The connection and meaning of the Arabic text have also been lost here by the <term>Urdu</term> translator. The <term>Arabic</term> original states, “He should not be talkative; he should say nothing from his own judgment beyond what was told to him, except in cases where he sees a benefit for the sender.” The <term>Urdu</term> version of the last sentence (which the translator <term>Ikram ’Alī</term> <note>Ikram 'Ali was a 19th-century scholar at Fort William College who translated these fables from Arabic into Urdu, often adding his own literary flair.</note> turned into two distinct sentences and embellished with his own ideas) should have been translated as: “He should say nothing of his own opinion except for what was told to him, unless he perceives a benefit for the sender in it.” <note>original Urdu: "Apnī rāʻe se bajuz uske kī jo usko batlāyā gayā hai kuchh na kahe illā jis men bhejne­wāle kī ṣalāḥ maʻlūm ho"</note>

<page-num>76</page-num>
<header>IKHWANU-S-SAFA</header>

In addition to this, they find many enjoyable kinds of food among humans. Furthermore, vices such as greed, avarice, and others that exist in men are found in the nature of dogs as well. This creates a stronger incentive for their close relationship. Other meat-eating <note>original: "carnivorous"</note> animals avoid these vices. The reason is that dogs eat meat regardless of how it is prepared: whether it is raw or cooked, "lawful" or "unlawful" <note>original: "lawful or unlawful"—referring to food that is permitted (*halal*) or prohibited (*haram*) by the *Shara’* (Islamic law).</note>, fresh or dry, salted or unsalted, and good or bad—they eat whatever they can get. 

And besides this, they eat all the various kinds of food eaten by humans: various fruits, vegetables, bread, legumes <note>original: "pulse"</note>, milk, curds, sweet and sour things, clarified butter <term>ghee: a form of highly clarified butter used in South Asian cooking</term>, oil, honey, sweets, flour, and more. They leave nothing uneaten. Wild meat-eating animals do not eat these things; indeed, they have no knowledge of them. 

Dogs are so filled with greed and avarice that they refuse to permit any other predatory animals to enter a town to find something to eat. If a fox or a jackal occasionally sneaks into a village at night to steal a hen <note>The original Arabic text specifies "a hen, a rooster, or a cat," whereas the Urdu translation used here says "a rat."</note>, some carrion, or a piece of bread, the dogs bark loudly, attack it, and eventually drive it away. Because of this same greed and selfishness, dogs are rendered vile, poor, and contemptible. <note>The original English translator noted a mistake in the Urdu text here. While the text says "how many are ruined," the intended meaning from the Arabic source is that the dogs' own greed causes them to live in a state of misery and low status.</note>

...and to Him we shall return," and he repeated this confession of faith <note>The Lion is reciting the "Istirja," a Quranic command (2:156) often recited by Muslims upon hearing bad news or during times of loss: "Verily, to Allah we belong and to Him we shall return."</note> several times.

The bear said to him: “Why does the separation of the cats and dogs from their own kind cause your Majesty such sorrow?”

The lion replied: “I do not mourn in the least for their absence itself. Rather, my sorrow is due to what the sages have declared: that no greater evil or mischief could befall kings in the governing of their realm than for any of their allies or soldiers to desert them and go over to the enemy. Such defectors will reveal to the enemy their vulnerable moments, as well as everything that is a strength, everything that is wrong, and every secret matter. After informing the enemy on all these points, they will show them unknown paths and numerous stratagems <note>original: "strata-gems"; tactical tricks or schemes.</note>.” These are all extremely great evils for kings and for their troops. May God ever withhold His blessing from these cats and dogs!”

## CHAPTER 12.

### ON THE MISSION OF THE SECOND ENVOY.

When the second messenger came to the bird-king, Shāh Murgh <note>original: "Shāh Murgh"; Persian for "King of Birds," often associated with the mythical Simurgh.</note>, and told his story, the king—upon hearing what had happened to the animals—ordered all the birds to attend to him. Consequently, various kinds of birds from the plains and the mountains, as well as many types of waterfowl, assembled in obedience to his command. They formed a multitude so vast that no one except God could count them. Shāh Murgh addressed them, saying:

“Men claim that all animals are their slaves and that they are the lords. For this reason, many animals are currently debating with the men in the presence of the King of the Genii <note>original: "Genii"; or Jinn, supernatural beings in Islamic theology who, like humans, possess free will and are judged by God.</note>.”

He then spoke to his minister, the peacock, asking: “Which of the birds excels in speech and eloquence, and is worthy of being sent there to debate with the men?”

The peacock replied, saying: “All the different families...

In obedience to this command, all the flies, mosquitoes, gnats, grasshoppers, fleas <note>The word “fleas” was added by the Urdu translator. The king shared the news he had received from the messenger and asked: “Which of you is qualified to go there, to side with the animals, and to debate with the men?”

The entire group of gnats said: "O King! We rely on the help of God, and we feel certain that, with His aid, we shall overcome them. In ancient times, there were great kings who were tyrants, and with God's help, we always triumphed over them; there has been repeated proof of this." The king said: "Tell me about those circumstances." # IKHWANU-S-SAFA <note>original: "IKHWANU-S-SAFA" — The Brethren of Purity, a 10th-century secret society in Iraq known for their encyclopedic work on science, philosophy, and religion.</note>

"...destroyed so great a king <note>original: "destroyed so great a king" — This refers to the legend of the tyrant Nimrod, who was supposedly killed when a gnat entered his nostril and reached his brain.</note>; and despite all his might, his dignity, and his power, they did him no good!"

The king remarked, "You speak the truth."

The wasps said: "When a man is wearing full armor and clutching his spear, his sword, his knife, and his arrows, and is ready for battle—if just one of us goes and bites him, piercing him with a sting (which is as sharp as a needle’s point), how miserable his condition becomes! His body swells; his arms and legs go limp; he is unable to move. Why, he even loses awareness of his sword and shield!" "True," said the king.

A fly said: "If a king of men sits on his throne in great splendor and ceremony, surrounded by chamberlains and guards showing extreme devotion and goodwill so that no annoyance or pain can reach him, and a single fly comes from his kitchen or latrine <note>original: "necessary" — an archaic term for a toilet.</note> with its whole body covered in filth, and sits on his person and clothes and pesters him, they are completely unable to protect him." The king replied, "What you say is true."

<margin>`</margin>
A <gloss>gnat</gloss> said: "If a man is sitting in any assembly, or behind a screen, or surrounded by curtains, and any one of our group goes and works its way into his clothes and stings him, how restless and angry he becomes! Yet his strength does him no good..."

In short, they provided him with everything necessary for the journey and took their leave of him. The wise bee departed and presented himself before the King of the Jinn, where many other kinds of animals were already gathered.

# CHAPTER XIV.

## THE MISSION OF THE FOURTH ENVOY.

When the fourth messenger went to the king of the birds of prey, the Anka <note>original: "’Ankā" — A mythical giant bird found in Arabic and Persian folklore, often identified with the Roc (Rukhkh) or Simurgh. According to the writer Al-Qazwini, it is the largest of all birds, capable of carrying off an elephant, and lives for 1,700 years. Like the phoenix, it is said to burn itself when its offspring matures.</note>, and described the situation of the animals, he also issued orders for all the birds of his class to be assembled. In accordance with this order, the vulture, the eagle, the hawk, the falcon, the kite, the owl, and the parrot <note>In historical Islamic classification, the parrot was often grouped with birds of prey because of its curved beak and claws.</note> arrived. In short, all such animals that have talons and curved <note>The translator notes that the word "curved" (original Arabic: "muḳawwasu-’l-murkur") is essential here to describe the beaks of these predatory birds.</note> beaks, and which feed on flesh, were instantly in attendance.

The Anka told them the story of the dispute—

->IKHWANU-S-SAFA<-

<note>7</note>

...of the animals. He then addressed his minister, the Falcon, saying, “Which of these animals is qualified for us to send there to oppose the humans and take part in the dispute on behalf of his brothers?”

The minister answered, “None of them, except for the owl, possesses the necessary qualifications for this task.”

The king asked, “Why is it that only the owl is suited for this task?” 

The minister replied, “The reason is that all birds of prey fear humans and fly from them; they do not understand human speech. However, the owl lives near their towns—even in the middle of their ruins. He practices self-denial <note>original: "abstinence"</note> and contentment to a degree not found in any other animal. He fasts all day and cries out in his fear of God. During the night, he is occupied with prayer and with waking those who have forgotten their Creator. # CHAPTER XV.

When the fifth envoy entered the presence of the king <note>original: "The Dragon"</note> of the sea creatures and informed him of the dispute, the king also called for a gathering of all his subjects.
