## CHAPTER XVI.

### DESCRIBING THE MISSION OF THE SIXTH ENVOY.

When the sixth messenger went to the king of the reptiles—the basilisk <note>A legendary reptile, often called the "king of serpents," said to have a lethal gaze.</note>—and described the events that had happened to the animals, the king immediately commanded the attendance of all crawling creatures. At once, all the snakes, scorpions, chameleons, lizards, iguanas,<sup>1</sup> spiders, lice, ants, earthworms—and in short, all those insects that are born in filth or crawl on the leaves of trees—presented themselves before the monarch. They gathered in such great numbers that no one but God could count them!

When the monarch saw their marvelous and strange forms, he was struck with wonder and remained silent for some time. As he scanned them closely, he saw a vast multitude of animals with small, feeble bodies and very limited senses and intelligence. He pondered deeply on what could be accomplished through their efforts, and he asked his—

The former is a lizard of the iguana kind, called in Hindī goh, in Arabic zabb, and in Persian sūsmār."</note> <note>Shakespeare’s Dictionary confuses the words *sūsmār* and *sūs*. The latter refers to a porpoise. The former is a lizard of the *iguana* kind, known as a *goh* in Hindi, a *zabb* in Arabic, and a *sūsmār* in Persian.</note>

the wazir <note>A high-ranking political advisor or minister</note>, who was a viper, replied: "Do you think that any of these are fit to be sent there by us for the purpose of debating and opposing the humans? For most of these animals are deaf, mute, and blind; they have absolutely no legs, no hair on their bodies, and neither beaks nor talons; and most of them are feeble and powerless."

In brief, the king experienced much anxiety and grief because of their state. He instinctively pitied them in his heart and wept with sorrow; looking towards heaven, he prayed to God as follows:
> "O You who are the Creator and Sustainer! You alone take compassion on the state of the weak! Of Your goodness and loving-kindness, look down on the condition of these creatures, for You are the Supremely Merciful."

Because of the monarch’s prayer, all the animals assembled there suddenly began speaking in the most eloquent manner. <note>original: ¹ The Arabic has: “The animals articulated with tongues of eloquence, ‘Amen! Oh, Lord of the beings of the universe!’”</note>

<header>THE BRETHREN OF PURITY <note>original: IKHWANU-S-SAFA</note></header>

God created the animals and made the form and figure of each one distinct. He gave strength to some and made others weak; on some He bestowed large bodies, and on others small. Yet, He has placed them all on an equal footing regarding His gifts and blessings. He provided each with the proper means of obtaining useful and beneficial things, as well as the right weapons for warding off injury. In this regard, all are equal. None enjoys any superiority over another. 

When He gave the elephant a massive body and great strength, He also provided him with two long tusks, which he uses to keep himself safe from the malice of predatory <note>original: rapacious</note> beasts; and by means of his trunk, he gains what he needs. And although He has given a tiny body to the gnat, He has provided it—as a form of compensation—with a pair of very delicate and nimble wings, which it uses to fly and save itself from its enemies. Regarding this gift, by which they obtain benefits and are protected from harm, the large and the small are all equal. 

In the same way, He has not excluded this group <note>The Cricket refers here to the insects and reptiles he represents.</note>—who appear, externally, to be without hair or feathers—from this gift. When God created them in this state, He made for them all the equipment <note>original: apparatus</note> by which they may acquire benefits and stay safe from injury. If your majesty will carefully consider their circumstances, you will see that those among them who are small and weak...

<header># THE BRETHREN OF PURITY <note>original: IKHWANU-S-SAFA</note></header>

results from it. The explanation for this is as follows:

When the Most High God created all the stars, He ordained the sun to be the lamp of the world and made its heat the source of life. This sun is to the whole world as the heart is to the body. Just as natural heat springs from the heart and spreads throughout the body as the source of life, so too does benefit result to all of creation from the heat of the sun. If, at any time, harm <note>original: mischief</note> and misfortune overtake some because of it, it is not appropriate for the Creator to destroy the sun on that account. To do so would deprive the greater part of the world of <gloss>general</gloss> good and complete benefit. 

The same situation applies to the planets Saturn and Mars, and to all the stars. Through them, well-being and prosperity come to the world, even though during certain unlucky <note>original: inauspicious</note> seasons, a few people experience harm from excessive heat or cold. 

In the same way, God (may He be exalted) sends rain clouds to every corner of the earth for the benefit of His creatures. This is true even though occasionally misfortunes happen to living things because of them, and the homes of some unfortunate <note>original: wretched</note> people are destroyed by excessive flooding <note>original: inundation</note>. 

Such is also the case with all carnivorous and herbivorous animals, as well as serpents, scorpions, fish, crocodiles, and insects. Some of these He has created in filth and decay <note>original: corruption</note> so that the atmosphere may be kept clear of rot <note>original: putrefaction</note>. <meta>This page continues the argument that even seemingly repulsive creatures like maggots and insects (referred to as "reptiles") serve a divine purpose by cleansing the environment and supporting the food chain.</meta>

<page-num>120</page-num>

<header># THE BRETHREN OF PURITY <note>original: IKHWANU-S-SAFA</note></header>

...life would perish instantly. This is why all these creeping creatures <note>original: reptiles; in this context, the term refers to any crawling thing, including maggots and insects</note> are commonly born in the shops of butchers and fishmongers and live in filth. When they are bred from filth, they transform whatever harmful <note>original: baneful</note> effects exist in that waste into nourishment <note>original: aliment</note>; because of this, the air becomes pure, and the world is kept safe from plague <note>original: pestilence</note>. Then, again, these small creatures serve as food for the larger ones that feed on them. In short, God (may He be exalted) has created nothing without a purpose.

It is only those who are unaware of these benefits who judge God, saying: “Why has He created these? They are of no benefit.” However, it is nothing but sheer ignorance and folly for them to pass disrespectful <note>original: impertinent</note> judgments on God’s actions. They have no knowledge of His skillful handiwork and His wondrous power.

I have heard that some foolish men imagine that the favors of God Most High do not extend beyond the orbit of the moon <note>original: lunar sphere; referring to the ancient belief that God’s direct providence only governed the "perfect" heavens, while the Earthly realm was left to decay</note>. If they were to carefully observe and reflect upon the circumstances of all existing things, they would learn that His goodness and loving-kindness include everything—both small and great. From the Source of the Generous Giver <note>original: Bounteous Bestower</note>, benefits flow to all creatures. Each one receives His bounty according to its own capacity.

## CHAPTER XVII.

### DESCRIPTIVE OF THE GATHERING OF THE REPRESENTATIVES OF THE ANIMALS.

In the morning, when the deputies <term>deputies: representatives sent to speak on behalf of others</term> of all the animals had arrived from every direction and were gathered together, and the monarch presided in the public court to decide the dispute at hand, the mace bearers <term>mace bearers: officials who carry a ceremonial staff (mace) as a symbol of authority and keep order in a court</term>, in obedience to their commands, proclaimed as follows: 

"Let all complainants <term>complainants: people who bring a formal legal charge or grievance against another</term> and seekers of justice who have suffered oppression come forward and be present; for the king is seated to settle the dispute, and the judges and legal experts <note>original: jurisconsults</note> are in attendance."

The moment they heard this, all the animals and men who had gathered there from every region formed themselves into rows and stood before the king, saluting him and calling for blessings upon him.

When the king observed them attentively, he saw that there were all kinds of animals present in vast numbers! For a moment, he remained speechless...

However, the spiritual forms are the movers, and the material forms are the moved. <note>The philosopher argues that spiritual realities provide the underlying energy or "motion" for physical things.</note> Furthermore, the forms lower in the scale of creation than these are without senses, motion, or speech;¹ while these material forms are perceived by the senses. <margin>A misprint in the Arabic text (which the translator might have easily detected if he had been more attentive) led to a nonsensical translation in Urdu. ## CHAPTER XVIII.

### CONTAINING AN ACCOUNT OF THE LION.

On the third day, when all the animals and men stood arranged in rows facing the King, he observed them all attentively and noticed the jackal standing at the front. “Who are you?” he asked.

The jackal replied, saying: “I am the representative <note>original: deputy</note> of the animals.”

“Who has sent you?” said the King.

“The monarch of the beasts of prey, *Abu-l-harith* <note>original: Abu-l-ḥariṣ; an Arabic honorific title for the Lion, meaning "Father of the Guard"</note>, has sent me,” he replied.

“In what country does he live?” asked the King, “and who are his subjects?”

He said: “He lives in forests and the wilderness, and all wild animals and four-legged beasts <note>original: quadrupeds</note> are his subjects.”

“Who are his supporters <note>original: auxiliaries</note>?” asked the King.

He answered: “Leopards, hog-deer <note>a small species of deer native to South and Southeast Asia</note>, antelopes, hares, foxes, and wolves are all his allies.”

“Describe his appearance and character,” said the King.

The jackal said: “He is of a larger build than any other beast of prey;¹ he possesses greater strength and is superior to all in awe-inspiring majesty. His chest—”

## CHAPTER XIX.

### DESCRIPTION OF THE BASILISK AND THE DRAGON.

AT THIS POINT, as the king was looking about him, a sound suddenly reached his ear. He turned his attention toward it and noticed a cricket shaking its wings and chirping with exceedingly soft notes! So he asked, “Who are you?” The cricket replied, saying: “I am the deputy of all the reptiles, and their king has sent me.” “Who is he?” asked the king, “and where does he live?” He replied: “His name is the Basilisk <note>A legendary reptile, often called the 'king of serpents,' said to have a lethal gaze or breath</note>; he lives on the summits of high mountains bordering the sphere of intense cold <note>original: kura-i-zamharīr</note>¹, where there are no clouds, no rain, and no vegetation, and where animals are destroyed by the severity of the cold.” “Who are his assistants <note>original: auxiliaries</note> and subjects?” asked the—

---

¹ The **sphere of intense cold** is said to be the highest portion of the **sphere of air** <note>original: kura-i-hawā</note>. In the ancient and medieval science used by the authors, these spheres are four in number: 1. The **sphere of earth** <note>original: kura-i-khāk</note>; 2. The **sphere of water** <note>original: kura-i-āb</note> or the surrounding ocean; 3. The **sphere of air**; 4. The **sphere of fire** <note>original: kura-i-ātish</note> or the surrounding ether. Beyond this is the first of the seven heavens, sometimes called the lunar sphere.

<margin>¹ This likely refers to the defensive wall <note>original: rampart</note> said to have been built by Alexander the Great. Yājūj and Mājūj are the names given by the Arabs to the Scythians of the far east, particularly those north of China. # THE BRETHREN OF PURITY <note>original: IKHWANU-S-SAFA; a 10th-century secret society of philosophers in Basra who produced an encyclopedia of sciences</note>

<margin>p</margin>
“To be brief, all the different kinds of aquatic animals fear and flee from him, while he fears none—except for a tiny creature about the size of a gnat. He is terrified of this small animal because when it stings him, its poison <note>original: venom</note> gradually spreads through his whole body until he finally dies. Then, all the aquatic animals gather together and feed on his flesh for a long time. Just as he eats other smaller animals, they eventually unite to eat him. 

The same situation applies to birds of prey and other birds as well. Sparrows and similar birds eat gnats and ants, while sparrow-hawks and falcons prey on those sparrows in turn. Then, even larger hawks and eagles hunt the sparrow-hawks and falcons. Finally, when these large birds die, all the tiny reptiles and worms feed on them. 

The same is true for mankind: humans eat the meat of antelopes, hog-deer <note>original: a species of small deer found in Southeast Asia</note>, goats, sheep, and birds; but when humans die, small worms feed on their bodies in the grave. This is the case for every living thing in the world. At one time the larger animals prey on the smaller, and at another time the smaller prey on the larger. Because of this, wise men <note>original: sages</note> have remarked that 'the death of one is the benefit <note>original: advantage</note> of another.' Regarding <note>original: à propos</note> this, God (may He be praised) declares: 'We cause these days of fortune and misfortune to rotate among people by turns;¹ but only the wise <note>original: learned</note> understand this.'” <margin>h.</margin>

# CHAPTER 20.

## AN ACCOUNT OF THE KING OF THE BEES.

As soon as the man had finished speaking, the king turned his attention toward the animals. Suddenly, a low, murmuring sound reached his ear. He looked and saw the leader of the bees, *Y'asub* <note>original: *Y’asub*; an Arabic term for the "king" or queen bee, often symbolizing a wise and noble ruler in Islamic literature</note>, flying in front of him and humming praises to God.

“Who are you?” asked the king.

“I am the king of all the winged insects,” he replied.

“How is it that you have attended in person?” the king asked. “Why did you not send a representative or a deputy from among your subjects and troops, as the other animals have done?”

The Bee King replied, “I felt compassion and tenderness for them, fearing that some trouble might happen to any of them if they came in my place.”

“No other animal possesses this quality,” said the king. “How is it that you have it?”

He answered, “God, in His goodness and loving-kindness, bestowed this quality upon me; and He has blessed me with many other outstanding gifts.”

...formed a perfect cube; the lower portion <note>original: "inferior"</note> is elongated; and the head is round. He has given us six exquisitely formed legs, proportionate in size like the sides of a hexagon. By means of these, we land and take flight, and we construct our honeycombs so skillfully that air cannot possibly enter them and cause discomfort to us or our young. Moreover, by using these legs, we gather the fruits, leaves, and blossoms of trees and store them in our honeycombs. He has also furnished us with two pairs of wings on our backs, by which we fly, and a venomous sting, with which we...

***

1. The Urdu word *Murabb'ā* <note>meaning "square"</note> is a mistake here. The word should have been *muk'ā'ab* <note>meaning "cube"</note>. The Arabic phrase is *Murabb'aan muk'ā'aban*, which I take to mean "A cube with square faces," or a perfect cube.

2. The original Arabic was not understood here by the Urdu translator. In the Arabic, we find: "The lower portion is conical and interlaced" <note>original: *mudammaj*</note>.

3. The Urdu word *chār* <note>four</note> should be *chha* <note>six</note>. If the Maulavi <note>a title for a Muslim scholar or teacher</note> had never studied the anatomy of a bee, he should have at least known from the comparison to the sides of a hexagon that the legs are six in number. The original Arabic says: "Four legs and two hands."

4. This is complete nonsense! The reader will see from the following translation how far the meaning of the Arabic has been changed. Beginning with the description of the bee's anatomy, the Urdu should have read: 

> "The description of it is this: that God Almighty has created three distinct, shaped joints in my body. The middle part is in the shape of a cube, of which all six faces are squares; the rear part is in the shape of a cone, which is partially tucked into the other <note>original: *mundarij*</note>; and the head is round and flat. In my middle section, He has created four feet and two hands, the sizes of which are proportionate to each other, like the sides of a hexagon within a circle. I use these hands and feet to help me rise, sit, descend, and climb. I lay the foundations of my homes in the shape of adjacent hexagons <note>original: *musaddasāt-i-ham pahlū*</note> so that air cannot enter them, which would harm my children and spoil my honey <note>original: *sharāb*, literally "wine" or "drink"</note>, which is my food and store. Through these same four feet and two hands, I collect the oily resins <note>original: *rutūbāt-i-duhniyah*</note> from the leaves, buds, and flowers of trees, from which my homes are built."

<summary>The King of the Bees describes the bee's anatomy—including its cubic body, hexagonal combs, and six limbs—as evidence of divine geometry. The translator provides extensive footnotes criticizing the previous Urdu translation's scientific and linguistic inaccuracies.</summary>

<keywords>bee anatomy, geometry, hexagon, Ikhwanu-s-Safa, translation critique, Islamic philosophy, divine design, entomology</keywords>

-> **IKHWANU-S-SAFA.** <-
<note>The title *Ikhwanu-s-Safa* translates to "The Brethren of Purity," a secret society of Muslim philosophers in 10th-century Iraq.</note>

...defend ourselves from injuries caused by enemies. He has given us a thin neck so that we can turn our heads to the right or left with the greatest ease. He has also placed two bright eyes¹—one on each side of the head—through whose light we perceive every object. He has provided us with a mouth to taste the sweetness of food and a pair of lips to gather things to eat. Moreover, He has given our stomachs digestive powers that convert the juices we gather into honey (just as He has equipped the udders of four-legged animals <note>original: "quadrupeds"</note> with organs that convert blood into milk). This honey alone serves as food for us and our young. To be brief, God (may He be exalted) has bestowed all these blessings upon us—how can we thank Him enough? Because of this, I have been moved with compassion and tenderness for my subjects. I have not sent <note>original: "deputed"</note> any of them here as my representative; instead, I felt it was right to take the trouble of coming myself.”

When Yāʻsūb had finished his speech <note>original: "oration"</note>, the king exclaimed: “Well done! A hundred times well done!"

***

¹ Translating from the Arabic, we find: “He made two bright eyes at the sides of the head, like two polished mirrors. He made these two eyes the instruments for us to distinguish visible objects, such as colors and forms, in both daylight and darkness. . . . He also gave us an open mouth with the sense of taste, which allows us to distinguish pure food from many other things to eat and drink. Moreover, He has given us two sharp lips, with which we gather delicate <note>original: "subtile"</note> juices from the fruits and blossoms of trees and the leaves of plants.”

-> **IKHWANU-S-SAFA** <-

“You are extremely eloquent! It is indeed true that God (may He be exalted) has not bestowed such gracious gifts on any other animal.” 

Then the king asked him: “Where do your subjects and troops live?”

He replied, saying: “On hills, mountains, and trees, and wherever they find convenience. Some, moreover, fly to the homes <note>original: "habitations"</note> of humans and make their dwellings there.” 

“And how do they escape their grasp <note>original: "clutches"</note>?” asked the king. 

He answered, saying: “They generally hide themselves to keep safe from them. But sometimes, when humans get them into their power, they harass <note>original: "molest"</note> them; indeed, they often <note>original: "nay, they not unfrequently"</note> destroy their structures, kill their young, take out the honey, and eat it themselves.”

“And how do you endure this oppression from them?” said the king. 

He replied: “We submit to it all patiently. Occasionally, however, our patience becomes exhausted and we leave their dwellings. Then they resort to <margin>Literally: "They bring forward, or they show."</margin> many tricks <note>original: "artifices"</note> to win us back <margin>Literally: "To make peace with us."</margin>, sending gifts of *itr* <term>*itr*: an essential oil or perfume, now commonly called attar.</term> and perfumes after us, and beating drums and tambours. In short, they offer us various kinds of choice gifts and thus satisfy us, for no resentment <note>original: "rancour"</note> or evil exists in our nature. We, too, become reconciled to them and return to their homes. And yet, they are still not satisfied with us; they make a claim against us without proof or evidence, saying: ‘We are their masters, and they are our slaves.’”

<summary>The King of the Bees describes how his subjects live in nature or alongside humans, enduring the theft of their honey and destruction of their hives. # CHAPTER 21.

->DESCRIBING THE LOYALTY OF THE JINN TO THEIR KING,<-
->AND TO THEIR CHIEFS.<-

And now *Ya’sub* <note>The King of the Bees.</note> said to the king: “Explain how the Jinn <note>original: "Genii"</note> obey their sovereign and their chiefs.” The king said: “They all show the most admirable obedience to those in authority over them and carry out every command that the king issues.” 

“Describe the matter in detail <note>original: "minutely"</note>,” said *Ya’sub*. 

So the king continued <note>original: "pursued"</note> as follows: “The race of the Jinn, like that of humanity, includes both good and bad, believers <note>Note in original: "Muslims."</note> and unbelievers. The good among them are loyal to a degree that even humans cannot reach, since the obedience of the Jinn is like that of the stars. Among the stars, the sun holds the position of king, and the other stars occupy the position of subjects and soldiers. 

For instance, Mars is the commander-in-chief; Jupiter is the judge; Saturn is the treasurer; Mercury is the prime minister; Venus is the consort <note>original: "concubine"</note>; the moon is the heir-apparent; and the other stars act as armies and subjects. Since they all follow the sun, they move—

Ya’sub <note>The King of the Bees</note> inquired where the stars had acquired such perfect <note>original: "exemplary"</note> obedience and regularity. The king answered, saying, "They received this as a gift from the angels, who make up the armies of God Most High and serve Him faithfully."

> "Similarly, the locust—when she has fed and grown fat in the spring—digs a hole in some soft ground, lays her eggs there, covers them with earth, and flies away. In the spring of the following year, when the air becomes mild, young locusts resembling worms come forth from those eggs and crawl on the earth, feeding on the greenery <note>original: herbage</note>. Then, through the wondrous power of God, life enters them again in the spring; they then build their nests, lay eggs, and breed young just as they did before.

> "In short, this is how all the insects of the earth bring forth and rear their young—through pure affection and tenderness, rather than because they expect any service in return. In this way, they are the opposite of humans, for men expect <term>filial piety</term> <note>original: filial piety; the duty of respect, obedience, and care that children owe to their parents</note> and gratitude from their offspring. Generosity, which is a defining quality of those who are truly great, is not found in humans. On what basis, then, do they boast of their superiority over us?

> "Furthermore, flies, mosquitoes, gnats, and other such insects lay eggs <note>The translator notes that this description of fly and gnat behavior differs from the original Arabic text.</note> and rear their young and make nests—not simply for their own benefit, but so that when they die, other insects may follow them and live in comfort."

Every one of these insects is fully certain that it will die; and when the hour of death arrives, they die with perfect resignation. Then, in the following year, God, through His omnipotence, brings them to life again. In short, these insects never refuse to believe in this cycle, whereas humans refuse to believe in the resurrection and the Day of Judgment. <note>See the Quran; chapter 79 (An-Naziat, "Those Who Pull Out"), verse 10: "They say, 'Will we indeed be returned to our former state?'"</note> 

If humans would only realize that these animals have knowledge of many more methods <note>original: "expedients"</note> for managing the affairs of this life—and greater trust regarding the state to which they return <note>referring to the afterlife or the spiritual return to their Creator</note>—they would not boast, saying: "We are the masters and the animals are our slaves."

# CHAPTER 22.

## DESCRIBING THE DISPUTE BETWEEN THE MEN, THE NIGHTINGALE, AND THE JACKAL.

When the deputy of the bees finished this speech <note>original: "oration"</note>, the King of the Genii <note>original: "Genii"; likely referring to the Jinn, supernatural beings in Islamic theology who, like humans, possess free will</note> was delighted with him and praised him. Then he turned toward the men and said: "You have heard all that he has said? Do you now have anything further to say in response?" 

One of their number, an Arab, spoke, saying: "We possess many virtues and good qualities by which our claim is established." 

"Mention them," said the king. 

He continued as follows: "Our lives are passed in great ease and comfort. We have within our reach many kinds of good things in the way of delicacies <note>original: "meats"; in older English, this often referred to food in general rather than just animal flesh</note> and drinks, which the animals do not even get a sight of. The kernels and pulp of fruits are eaten by us, whereas the animals devour the rinds and seeds <note>original: "stones"</note>. In addition to this, we eat various kinds of fine foods, such as *shīrmāl* <note>original: "kneaded with milk"; from the Persian *shīr* (milk) and *māl* (to knead). It is a round, saffron-flavored flatbread made with milk</note>, *bāḳir khānī* <note>a thick, spiced flatbread</note>, *gāwdīda* <note>literally "cow's eye"; a type of biscuit or small pastry</note>, *gāwzabān* <note>literally "cow's tongue"; a tongue-shaped pastry</note>, and *kutiche* <note>likely a variation of "kulcha," a soft leavened bread or small cake</note>,

---

¹ *Shīrmāl*, literally, "kneaded with milk"; (from *shīr*, "milk" and *māl*, the imperative of *mālīdan*, "to knead") is a round, flat cake made of flour.

<summary>A human representative, an Arab man, argues for human superiority over animals by highlighting the luxury, comfort, and sophisticated variety of foods and pastries that humans enjoy.</summary>
<keywords>King of the Genii, Jinn, Arab, human superiority, animal dispute, culinary culture, shirmal, bakir khani, delicacies</keywords>

--- Page 183 ---
<meta>This page continues the list of delicacies and fine foods that humans claim as evidence of their superiority over animals. It specifically describes various types of breads, rice dishes (pulaos), and sweetmeats common in Indo-Persian cuisine.</meta>

<page-num>165</page-num>

# IKHWANU-S-SAFA <note>original: "The Brethren of Purity"</note>

*...mutanjan, zerbiriyan, muza’far, shīrbiranj, kabāb, kormā, būrānī, firni, milk, yogurt <note>original: "tyre"</note>, clarified butter, and various kinds of sweets, such as halwā sohan, jalebī, and laddū,*

and ghee <note>clarified butter</note>, kneaded with milk. *Bakir Khani* is a cake very similar to *Shirmal*, but it contains more milk and ghee, making it crumblier <note>original: "shorter"</note> and richer. It takes its name from a man named Bakir Khan, who is said to have introduced it to India from Persia. *Gawdida* <note>Persian: "cow's eye"</note> is a small loaf or bun that is thought to look like a cow's eye. *Gawzaban* <note>Persian: "cow's tongue"</note> is a type of bread shaped like a cow’s tongue. *Kulicha*—literally meaning "a little circle or disk"—is a type of cake similar to *shirmal* and *bakirkhani*. 

*Mutanjan* is a type of *pulao* <note>a seasoned rice dish</note> that includes both sweet and acidic ingredients in addition to the usual components. *Zer biryan* (properly *zar biryan*, which through common use became *zir biryan* and then *zer biryan*) is a type of *pulao* featuring a large amount of saffron, which gives it a deep yellow <note>original: "zar," meaning gold or yellow</note> color. *Muza'far* is also a *pulao* prepared with saffron. *Shirbiranj*—properly *shir o biranj*, meaning "milk and rice"—also known as *khir*, is a dish made of rice boiled in milk. 

*Burani* is a dish said to have been invented by a woman named Buran. It is prepared as follows: the fruit of the eggplant <note>original: "baingan"</note> is sliced lengthwise, seasoned well with salt and chilies, fried in ghee, and then steeped in yogurt <note>original: "tyre (dahi)"</note> for some time. This dish is then eaten with *pulao*. 

*Firni* is also rice boiled in milk. It differs from *khir* because the rice is ground into a fine powder before being boiled. *Halwa sohan*, or *halwa-i-sohan lal*, is named after a man named Sohan Lal, its first creator. It is a sweetmeat similar to toffee, though it contains more ingredients and is richer. 

*Jalebi* (derived from the Arabic *zalubiya*) is a sweetmeat made of leavened dough, sugar, and a large amount of ghee. The dough is made very thin and poured through a fine cloth with holes in it. As the dough falls into the hot fat, it is formed into a series of concentric circles joined by cross-bars; the outermost circle is about two or three inches wide. *Imrati* is a richer version of *jalebi*. It is considered so delicious that it is compared to nectar <note>original: "amrit," the drink of the gods in Hindu mythology</note>. 

*Laddu* is a sweetmeat made of fine flour (made from legumes or chickpeas), loaf sugar, and ghee. It is shaped into a ball ranging from one to three inches in diameter. *Pera* is a sweetmeat made from cream and loaf sugar. *Barfi*—named after the Persian word *barf*, meaning "ice" or "snow"—is also made of cream and sugar. It is shaped like a small brick and topped with a thin layer of edible silver leaf; it looks somewhat like a piece of ice, which is how it got its name. *Lauziyat*—from the Arabic *lauz*, meaning "an almond"—refers to almond confections or any sweetmeat containing almonds.

<summary>This page provides a detailed glossary of various Persian and Indian breads, rice dishes, and sweets, explaining their ingredients, preparation methods, and the etymology of their names.</summary>
<keywords>Mughal cuisine, Indian sweets, Persian influence, food history, pulao, jalebi, barfi, Bakir Khani</keywords>

# THE BRETHREN OF PURITY <note>original: "IKHWANU-S-SAFA"</note>

...milk fudge <note>original: "pēre"</note>, condensed milk sweets <note>original: "barfī"</note>, fried syrup rings <note>original: "imratī"</note>, almond confections <note>original: "lauziyāt"</note>, and so on. For our amusement and diversion, we have dances <note>original: "nāch" (an Indian performance where a dancing girl sings and moves to music simultaneously)</note> and fun, and merriment, and tales, and stories. We wear various kinds of costly apparel and jewels. We spread rugs and carpets, white and colored floor-cloths, and many other types of floor coverings. 

Where can the animals obtain such articles? They always feed on the vegetation of the forest, and, like slaves, pass their days and nights in a state of utter nakedness <note>original: "nang dharang," a phrase where both words mean "body," emphasizing being completely bare</note> in the midst of toil and labor. These things prove that we are the masters and they are the slaves.”

The nightingale, acting as the deputy of the birds, was sitting on the branch of a tree in front of the group. He addressed the king, saying: “Does this man, who boasts of their varieties of food and drink, not know that in reality these things are the source of great affliction and trouble for them?” 

“Explain how this happens,” said the king. 

The nightingale proceeded: “Because for the sake of this ease, they must endure much toil and trouble—such as digging the ground, ploughing...

<page-num>167</page-num>

# THE BRETHREN OF PURITY <note>original: "IKHWANU-S-SAFA"</note>

...drawing water, filling water jars, sowing seed, reaping, weighing, grinding, lighting fires in ovens, cooking, haggling with butchers for meat, keeping accounts with food sellers <note>The original term "vendors of eatables" refers to merchants</note>,¹ toiling to accumulate wealth, acquiring knowledge of sciences and arts, exhausting their bodies,² traveling to distant lands, and standing with folded hands in the presence of the powerful for the sake of a couple of small coins <note>original: "pice," a copper coin of low value in British India</note>. 

In short, these are the labors and exertions by which they gather wealth and property; and, after their death, these riches fall to others. If they have acquired wealth by lawful means, even then they will have to give an account for it <note>This refers to the belief that on Judgment Day, one must explain how every penny was earned and spent</note>; and if they gained it by other means, punishment and misery will be their portion. 

We, however, are exempt from such toil and trouble, since our food consists simply of herbs and plants. We enjoy, without labor or effort, whatever springs from the earth. We eat various kinds of fruits which God (may He be exalted) has, by His Almighty Power <note>original: "Omnipotence"</note>, caused to grow for our use, and we are forever busy returning Him [thanks]...

---

¹ The term "grocer" is not the exact equivalent of the Urdu *baniyā*. A *baniyā* sells grain, flour, lentils <note>original: "pulse"</note>, meal, oil, clarified butter <note>original: "ghī"</note>, salt, and raw sugar—and nothing else, I believe. 

² This repetition in the text is the result of a faulty translation. The Urdu should have read: "And the teaching of crafts that tire the body, and the teaching of those tasks which are difficult for the soul, and the teaching of accounting in commerce, and the teaching of traveling back and forth on long and distant journeys for the pursuit of wealth, property, and necessities." <note>original Urdu: "Aur badanōṇ kī thakādenewālī ṣana’tōṇ kā sikhlānā aur un kāmōṇ ka jo jānōṇ par dushwār haiṇ sikhlānā aur tijāratōṇ meṇ ḥisābōṇ kā sikhlānā aur māl o natā’ aur ḥawāij kī just-o-jū ke liye safarhā-i-dūr o darāz meṇ āmad o raft kā sikhlānā."</note>

# THE BRETHREN OF PURITY <note>original: "IKHWANU-S-SAFA"</note>

...thanks. The thought of searching <note>The original phrase is "Fikr-o-talāsh," meaning the worry and the search</note> for food never enters our minds; instead, wherever we wander, through God's bountiful kindness, we find all that we desire. These men, however, are constantly worried with anxiety about food.

"Again, as they eat many different kinds of food, they suffer various kinds of torments, being constantly afflicted with lingering diseases: fever, headache, cholera, delirium, palsy <note>original: "palsy," a historical term for tremors or loss of motor function</note>, paralysis, ague <note>original: "ague," a fever involving shivering fits, often malaria</note>, cough, jaundice, <note>2</note> hectic fever <note>a persistent fever often associated with tuberculosis</note>, boils, pimples, itch, ringworm, scrofula <note>a form of tuberculosis affecting the lymph nodes of the neck</note>, stomach cramps <note>original: "gripes"</note>, diarrhea, syphilis, gonorrhea, elephantiasis, and polyps. In short, many kinds of diseases befall them, and they are perpetually running to physicians for remedies. Yet they impudently assert that they are the lords and we are the slaves!"

The man replied, saying: "Diseases are in no way unique to us; animals, too, are commonly afflicted by them."

The animal representative rejoined: "The animals that fall sick do so simply through associating with you. Dogs, cats, pigeons, fowls, and others that are captives among you are not allowed to eat and drink according to their own nature, and therefore they fall sick. But those animals that roam at full liberty in the jungles are free from all ailments, since their feeding-time is..."

...and is not the product of any of your own craftsmanship.

Why then do you boast? Only fruits and grain remain, and we share in eating these with you. From ancient times until now, our ancestors and yours shared them regularly. When your first ancestors, Adam and Eve, lived in the Garden of Paradise and ate the fruits there without labor or toil—being free from all anxiety and trouble—our ancestors also shared those comforts with them there.

When your great ancestors, through the deception of their enemy <note>The "enemy" refers to Satan or Iblis, who tempted Adam and Eve</note>, forgot God’s warning and desired a single grain of wheat <note>original: "coveted a single grain"</note>, they were expelled from there. The angels carried them down and placed them in a location where there was not even any vegetation <note>original: "there was not even any vegetation"</note>. What chance was there, then, of finding any fruit? In this state of misery, they mourned for a long time. At last, their repentance was accepted, and God forgave their sin. He sent an angel who came and taught them how to dig the ground, how to sow, how to grind corn, how to cook, and how to make clothes. In short, such labors occupied them day and night. When their offspring multiplied, and...

# THE BRETHREN OF PURITY <note>original: "IKHWANU-S-SAFA"</note>

...began to inhabit all places, jungles as well as towns, then they commenced mistreating <note>original: "ill-using"</note> those who lived on the earth. They forcibly took over their homes, seized many of them, and made them captives. Many fled; to capture them, the humans built many kinds of snares and nets and pursued them. Finally, things have reached such a point that you now stand up boldly, asserting your superior glory and dignity, and are ready for argument <note>original: "disputation"</note> and quarreling!

"And, regarding what you say about holding parties—entertaining yourselves with music and dancing, spending your days in joy and pleasure, wearing expensive clothing and various kinds of jewelry, and enjoying many other things that are beyond our reach—all of this is true. But for every one of these joys, you have a balancing <note>original: "counterbalancing"</note> sorrow from which we are exempt. As a trade-off for your wedding feasts, you sit in houses of mourning; as an equivalent for your happiness, you experience sadness; instead of music and laughter, you weep and endure grief; instead of grand buildings, you lie in the dreary grave; instead of your jewelry, you wear iron collars on your necks, manacles on your hands, and chains <note>original: "fetters"</note> on your feet; and to balance out your high praises <note>original: "eulogies"</note>, you are subjected to insults <note>original: "satires"</note>. In short, for every joy, you also have a corresponding sorrow; whereas we are safe from these troubles.

<summary>In this passage, the animal representative argues that human "superiority" is a result of violent conquest and that every human pleasure is offset by a corresponding misery, such as grief, imprisonment, or death.</summary>

# THE BRETHREN OF PURITY <note>original: "IKHWANU-S-SAFA"</note>

...since such trials and hardships <note>original: "afflictions"</note> are only suitable for those who are miserable slaves. <note>The original Arabic text emphasizes: "For those alone who are miserable slaves."</note>

"Instead of your cities and buildings, we have the freedom of this vast <note>original: "spacious"</note> plain. We fly wherever we please <note>original: "list"</note>, from the earth to the sky. We feed without any trouble on the fresh grass <note>original: "herbage"</note> by the riverbanks, and we eat all permitted <note>original: "lawful"</note> food without having to work or struggle for it. We drink pure water without anyone stopping us, and without needing ropes, buckets, water-bags, or drinking cups. You, however, need all these things; you carry them on your shoulders from place to place to sell them. <note>The original English translation is slightly unclear, but the Arabic source clarifies that the humans are selling the equipment (ropes, bags, etc.), rather than the water itself.</note> You are constantly burdened with hard work and trouble. Now, all of these are the signs of slaves. How then can you prove that you are the masters and we are the slaves?"

The king then spoke to the humans' representative, asking, "Now, what further response do you have to make?" 

He answered, "We possess many excellent and distinguished gifts which prove our case." 

"Describe them," said the king.

One of the men, a Jew, spoke, saying, "God has given <note>original: "bestowed"</note> various great gifts to us. He has granted us the blessings of religion, prophecy, and His revealed Word. He has shown us what is permitted and what is forbidden—what is good and what is evil—"

# THE BRETHREN OF PURITY <note>original: "IKHWANU-S-SAFA"</note>

—and has set us apart for admission into the Garden of Paradise. All these distinguished gifts clearly show that we are the masters <note>original: "lords"</note> and the animals are the slaves."

The representative <note>original: "deputy"</note> of the birds replied, "If you consider this carefully, you will see that these things are actually burdens <note>original: "afflictions"</note> and punishments for you."

"How are these burdens?" the King asked.

The bird replied: "God—may He be exalted—has commanded all these acts of worship so that their sins may be erased <note>original: "blotted out"</note> and so that they do not wander from the right path. And so God declares in the Quran <note>original: "Korān"</note>, saying: 'Good deeds wipe out evil deeds.'¹ If they did not follow these religious laws <note>original: "ordinances"</note>, they would be disgraced in God's eyes. It is out of fear of this disgrace that they pray constantly. In contrast, we animals are free from sin and have no need for the religious duties <note>original: "devotional offices"</note> that this man takes such pride in."

"Furthermore, God Almighty sent prophets to those who are unbelievers, idolaters <note>original: "polytheists"</note>, and sinners—those who do not worship Him but commit lawless and sinful acts day and night. Meanwhile, we—"

# THE BRETHREN OF PURITY <note>original: IKHWANU-S-SAFA</note>

—are free from such idolatry <note>original: polytheism</note> and sin. We believe God is one and has no equals <note>original: associates</note>; and we are always busy praising Him. Furthermore, prophets and apostles are like doctors <note>original: physicians</note>¹ and astrologers. Only those who are sick and suffering need doctors, and only the unfortunate seek help from astrologers. <note>¹ The original text clarifies that "Prophets and apostles are doctors and astrologers for souls."</note> Again, ritual washings <note>original: ablutions</note> and purifications are commanded <note>original: enjoined</note> because you are in a constant state of impurity <note>original: uncleanness</note>. You spend all your time in adultery and sexual immorality <note>original: fornication</note>, and your bodies generally have an offensive smell. This is why purifications are required for you. Now, we avoid these things. Prayers and fasts are commanded so that your sins may be forgiven. We commit no sin, so why should these duties be mandatory for us?

Charity <note>original: alms</note> and religious taxes² <note>original: poor-rates</note> are necessary because you pile up wealth through both legal and illegal means, and you give nothing to those who have a right to your help. <note>² This refers to Zakāt, the portion of property a person gives to the poor to purify their wealth. This is a mandatory obligation in Islam, usually consisting of a fortieth part (2.5%) of one's wealth, provided the person has held that amount for nearly a year.</note> If you spent a portion of your wealth on the poor and needy, why would these religious taxes be made mandatory for you? But we show kindness and pity to our own species and never hoard anything through greed <note>original: avarice</note>.”

“And as for your claim that the Most High—”

<summary>The animal representative argues that religious laws like prayer, fasting, and ritual washing are remedies for human sins and impurities rather than marks of superiority. He contends that animals are naturally sinless and generous, making such laws unnecessary for them.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the speech of the animal representative, who argues that while humans require prophets and written laws due to their spiritual blindness, animals receive direct divine inspiration and instinct from God.</meta>

# THE BRETHREN OF PURITY <note>original: IKHWANU-S-SAFA</note>

God has revealed for your sake scriptures concerning what is permitted and what is forbidden, legal limits, and laws of retribution; these are for your instruction because your hearts are blind. Because of your ignorance, you cannot perceive what is beneficial or what is harmful, so you require teachers. In contrast, God grants us all knowledge directly, without the need for prophets. Thus He Himself declares, saying: “Your Lord directed the bee to build its home in the mountains;”¹ and in another place: “Every animal knows its prayer and its praise.”²

And in another place, He declares as follows: “God sent a raven to scratch the ground, to show Cain how to bury his brother’s corpse.” On seeing it, Cain exclaimed: “Woe is me! Am I not even able to be like this raven and bury my brother’s body?” And so he became one of those who feel regret.³

And regarding your claim that you go to mosques and Sufi lodges⁴ for congregational prayers, we have no...

***

1. Literally, “two shawls,” as a pair of shawls was always worn together. *Kamkhwab* <note>literally, “little sleep” or “little nap”</note> is a rich silk embroidered with gold or silver thread. *Mashru* <note>literally, “prescribed by religious law” or *Shara*</note> is a fabric made of a mix of silk and cotton. Muslims are not allowed to perform formal prayers while wearing a garment of pure silk. *Sahn* is a type of sturdy cloth resembling “sheeting.” It used to be manufactured extensively some time ago in Benares and Jaunpur. The lexicographer John Shakespear spells it *sahan* and seems to be uncertain of this spelling. *Jajam* (as it is commonly pronounced in India; *jajim* according to Johnson’s Persian Dictionary and other vocabularies) is a stamped or patterned floor-cloth. *Chandni*—so called for its whiteness—is a floor-cloth that covers an entire room, usually with another floor-cloth or carpet underneath.

# CHAPTER XXIII.

->ON THE DISCUSSION BETWEEN THE HUMAN BEINGS AND THE PARROT.<-

In the morning, all the humans and animals appeared in the Court of Justice. The king addressed the humans, saying: "If you have any other proofs to support your claim, state them now." 

The Persian representative said: "We possess many noble qualities <note>original: properties</note> by which our claim of superiority is established." 

"Mention them," said the king. 

He continued as follows: "Among our people there are kings, *viziers* <note>original: wazīrs; high-ranking political ministers or advisors</note>, viceroys <margin>or Governors</margin>, secretaries, finance managers <margin>or Accountants-General</margin>, revenue collectors, generals <note>original: Faujdār. The translator notes that while lexicons differ, the Arabic equivalent *kuwwād* indicates military commanders or generals.</note>, ushers <note>original: naḳīb; an official who announces guests or leads processions.</note>, mace-bearers <term>Mace-bearer: an officer who carries a ceremonial staff of authority before a high-ranking official.</term>, servants, and helpers <note>original: Yar o Madadgār. The translator suggests "Footmen and Soldiers" (Piyāde aur Sipāhī) would be a more accurate modern rendering of the original intent.</note>. # IKHWANU-S-SAFA <note>Arabic: "The Brethren of Purity." This refers to a 10th-century secret society of philosophers in Basra who wrote an influential encyclopedia of the same name.</note>

...good habits in common with them. For among our tribes as well, there are sovereigns, chiefs, subjects, and armies. In fact, our rulers are superior to the kings of men in their governance and rule; for human kings promote the welfare of their subjects and armies solely for their own interests and benefit. Once they have achieved their goals, they pay no attention at all to the condition of their armies and subjects. However, this is not the way of true princes.

To govern and rule properly, it is necessary that a king should at all times exercise mercy and kindness toward his armies and subjects. Just as God the Most High always deals mercifully with His servants, so it <term>behooves</term> <note>original: "behoves"; meaning it is necessary or appropriate.</note> every king to regard his subjects with affection. 

Now, the rulers of the different classes of animals always regard their troops and subjects with mercy and kindness. Similarly, the kings of the ants and the birds also constantly attend to the well-being and order of their subjects. Whatever kind treatment and good they bestow upon them, they do not desire any return for it. Nor do they look for any benefit from their offspring in return for raising them, as humans do—who, after bringing up their children, make them serve them. 

Animals give birth to young and nourish them, and then seek nothing from them in return; instead, they raise and nourish them through pure affection. In this, they steadily follow the example of God; for He forms His creatures and...

<summary>The parrot argues that animal rulers and parents are superior to humans because they provide care and leadership out of pure altruism and divine affection, whereas humans act out of self-interest.</summary>

# IKHWANU-S-SAFA <note>Arabic: "The Brethren of Purity." This refers to a 10th-century secret society of philosophers in Basra who wrote an influential encyclopedia of the same name.</note>

...provides them with sustenance and does not expect gratitude from them. If these evil deeds were not to be found among men, why would God command them to "be grateful to me and to your parents"? <note>This refers to the Quran, Chapter 31 (Surah Luqman), verse 14. The text cites verse 13, but the command is in the following verse.</note> He has issued no such command to our young ones, since they do not practice ingratitude <note>original: *kufr* or *kufran*; the author notes that while *kufr* usually means "infidelity" or "unbelief," the original Arabic intended *kufran*, meaning "ingratitude."</note> and disobedience.”

When the parrot reached this point of his speech, the sages of the Genii <note>original: "Genii"; referring to the Jinn, supernatural beings in Islamic theology who, like humans, possess free will and are subject to God's judgment.</note> observed, “This speaker speaks the truth.” The men became ashamed and hung their heads; not one of them made any answer. 

Meanwhile, the king asked a sage, “Who are these kings he has spoken of, who deal mercifully and tenderly with their troops and subjects?” 

The sage replied, saying, “By these kings, he means the angels. For as many classes and species of animals as there are, all have angels appointed over them by God, who watch over and guard each kind. Moreover, the bands of angels also have chiefs and rulers, who each rule their own band with mercy and kindness.” 

“Where did the angels acquire this mercy and tenderness?” asked the king. 

The sage replied, saying, “They obtained this benefit from the mercy of the Most High God. For He shows mercy to His creatures in such great measure that the mercy of any other being in the universe does not attain...

# IKHWANU-S-SAFA <note>Arabic: "The Brethren of Purity." A 10th-century philosophical society.</note>

...not even to the hundred-thousandth part <note>The original Arabic text states "to the millionth part of it."</note> of it. The king asked, “Who is the chief of the angels who are appointed to watch over humans?”

The <term>rational soul</term> continues within <term>Adam</term>’s race to this day, just as the physical form of <term>Adam</term> continues in his offspring to the present time. It is with this form that they are born..."

"What is the reason," asked the king, "that angels and the different forms of life <note>referring here to spiritual or non-physical beings</note> are not perceptible?" 

The sage replied, saying: "Because they are composed of light and are subtle essences, they cannot be perceived by the physical senses. But prophets and saints perceive them because of the purity of their hearts, for their awareness is free from the darkness of ignorance. They are on their guard against the slumber of thoughtlessness and resemble spirits and angels; thus, they perceive them, hear their speech, and share that knowledge with their own kind."¹

Upon hearing this speech, the king said to the sage: "May God reward you well!" Then he turned to the parrot and said: "Finish your discourse." 

The parrot continued: "Regarding what this man states² about his race comprising many artisans and craftsmen, there is no reason for superiority in that, since among us animals, too, certain species possess those arts just as they do. For instance, the honeybee possesses greater skill in building and repairing than their builders and mathematicians <note>original: "geometricians"</note> do. She constructs—"

<meta>This page continues the parrot's argument on behalf of the animal kingdom, specifically focusing on the natural craftsmanship of "feeble" insects like spiders and silkworms to challenge the human claim of superiority based on technology and art.</meta>

# IKHWANU-S-SAFA <note>Arabic: "The Brethren of Purity." A 10th-century philosophical society.</note>

...she builds her home without mud, brick, lime, or plaster; and she requires no ruler or compass to draw lines and circles, whereas men must have materials and tools. The case is similar with the spider, which is the weakest of insects. Yet in spinning and weaving, she is wiser than human weavers. First, she draws out a strand from her own secretions <note>original: "slime"</note>; then she makes the likeness of a number of threads, and after that arranges them from above, leaving a small space open in the center to catch flies. Now, in this skillful work, she needs no tools; whereas weavers can weave nothing without instruments.¹

The same is true of the silkworm, which is an extremely weak creature. And yet she possesses greater skill and knowledge than human artisans. When she is full from feeding <note>original: "satiated with pasture"</note>, she withdraws to her shelter and first spins, from her secretions, something resembling fine threads; then she arranges and strengthens—

***

¹ The whole of this paragraph, describing the spider’s method of spinning, is poorly translated in the Urdu version. As far as the words *is clever* <note>original Urdu: "hoshyār hai"</note>, the substance of the original Arabic is given after a fashion; from that point on, the Urdu should have read as follows: 

"This is because when weaving a web, she first stretches a thread from one wall to another, or from one tree to another, or from one side of a stream to the other, without having to walk on the water or fly through the air. Then she walks along this and builds the framework <note>original: "tāna," referring to the warp threads in weaving</note> of her web in straight lines—it looks as though they are the support ropes of a standing tent. Then she weaves the cross-threads <note>original: "bānā," referring to the woof or weft</note> in a circular pattern and makes an open circle in the center so that she can hunt flies there. She does all this without a spindle, spinning wheel, loom, bobbins, comb, or any of the other tools that your weavers require." 

The next paragraph, too, which describes the silkworm, is hardly better translated than the one preceding it.

### IKHWANU-S-SAFA <note>Arabic: "The Brethren of Purity." A 10th-century philosophical society.</note>

...it from the outside, so that no air or water enters it; and she sleeps inside, according to her custom. She has acquired all these skills without instruction from her parents or from a teacher. She has no need for needles and thread, unlike human tailors and menders who can make nothing without such tools.

Furthermore, the swallow builds her nest suspended in the air beneath the roofs of houses, and she requires no ladder or equipment to climb and reach it. Likewise, the white ant <note>term: termite</note> builds her nest without mud and without water. She lacks for nothing. In short, all birds and animals build their homes and nests and raise their young with greater intelligence and skill than men. 

For example, consider the ostrich, which is a combination of a bird and a land animal <note>original: "beast"</note>—with what excellence she raises her young! When twenty or thirty of her eggs have accumulated, she divides them into three groups: one part she buries underground, another she leaves in the sun, and a third she places under her wings.¹ When the young ones are hatched, she digs into the ground to find nourishment for them, pulling out worms and insects to feed her young.² No woman nourishes her children in such a way; but

***

¹ <note>original: "Translating from the original, we have 'One-third she buries under ground; one-third she leaves in the sun; and one-third she hatches.'"</note>

² <note>The Urdu translator misunderstood the original text here. The original Arabic describes a different method of feeding: when the chicks hatch, the mother breaks the eggs that were left in the sun. The sun's heat has melted the contents into a thin liquid, which the young chicks then drink as their first food. (Original Urdu: "When she brings out her young, she breaks the eggs that were in the sun, taking their moisture—which the sun has melted and thinned...")</note>

...have no knowledge of their own affairs, such as what will happen to them or what the future holds for their children. Only those who are wicked and unjust—such as cruel and tyrannical kings—put their trust in the predictions of astrologers. Such rulers deny the existence of a future state <note>original: "future state"; referring to the afterlife or the day of judgment</note> and remain ignorant of the fact that all events are predestined by God.

This was the case with **Nimrod** and **Pharaoh** <note>Nimrod was a legendary Mesopotamian king, and Pharaoh refers to the king of Egypt during the time of Moses; both are used here as archetypes of tyrannical rulers who opposed God's prophets.</note>, who, because of the forecasts made by astrologers, had hundreds, or rather thousands, of children slaughtered. They believed that the order of the world depended on the seven planets and the twelve signs of the zodiac. They did not realize that nothing happens without the decree of that God who created those very signs and planets. It is a true saying that **"no human planning can prevail against God's fixed decrees."** In the end, only that which God had ordained came to pass.

The account is as follows: The astrologers informed **Nimrod** that a child would be born during his reign who, once he grew up and achieved great status, would overthrow the religion of the idol-worshippers. <header># IKHWANU-S-SAFA <note>Arabic: "The Brethren of Purity," a 10th-century secret society of Muslim philosophers.</note></header>

"...immediately, pray to God, humble yourselves, mourn, and cry out to Him. Because of the sincerity of your prayers <note>original: "petitions"</note>, He will deliver you from that calamity." 

"From Adam to Muhammad Mustafa <note>original: Mohammad Mustafá. "Mustafa" is an honorary title for the Prophet Muhammad meaning "the Chosen One."</note> (may God bless and save him), this was the path they followed. That is to say, whenever any trouble occurred, each prophet used to give this exact same command to his people. Therefore, it is right to act in this way regarding the decrees of the stars, rather than following the way modern astrologers mislead people. For they abandon God and rely instead on the rotations of the heavenly spheres <note>original: "celestial orbs."</note>."

"To recover from sickness, one should also first turn to God. For complete restoration to health is obtained through His favor and mercy alone. One should not turn away from the threshold of the true Healer <note>In this context, the "actual Healer" refers to God.</note> to rely only on physicians. There are some men who, at the first sign of disease, go to doctors; when they receive no benefit from their remedies, they turn away in despair and finally seek God. In fact, they often write out descriptions of their cases with earnest pleas and many tears, hanging them on the walls and pillars of mosques, and then God restores them to health."

"In the same way, one should turn to God regarding the influences of the stars, and not act according to the misleading advice of astrologers. For instance, there was a king whom..."

<summary>The text argues that sincere prayer and humility before God are the only effective remedies for disaster and illness, criticizing those who trust solely in astrologers or doctors over divine mercy.</summary>

...the astrologers reported that a certain disastrous event <note>original: "calamitous event"</note> would occur in his city, which the residents had much to fear.¹ So the king asked them how it would happen, but they could not provide the specific details. However, they did say this much: that in a specific month, on a specific day, this event would take place. 

The king asked his people, "What plan should we adopt to prevent <note>original: "avert"</note> this evil?" 

Those who were religious and God-fearing said: "It would be best for the king and all the city's inhabitants, both great and small, to leave the city on that day and stay in the open fields. There, they should earnestly and tearfully beg God to prevent this disaster; perhaps <note>original: "peradventure"</note> God will rescue them from it." 

Following their advice, the king went out and stayed outside the city that day, and many people accompanied him. They began praying to God to save them from this trouble and stayed awake all night long. 

But some of the people felt no alarm despite the prediction of the astrologers and remained inside the city. During the night, the rain poured down heavily; since the city was situated on low ground, the water was drawn into it from all sides so that the city was submerged. Everyone who had remained...

"How does this happen?" the man asked. "Explain yourself!" 

He replied, "Because they turn people away from the path ordained by God. Through their disagreements, they make the laws of religion ineffective. Their opinions and beliefs are all at odds with one another. Some claim the universe is the most ancient thing; others believe matter is; some try to prove the antiquity of form. <note>1 See the Quran, chapter 7 (*Al-A’raf*), verse 64. (The text cites verse 62, following older numbering systems).</note>
<note>2 In Aristotelian philosophy, every material object is believed to have four causes: 
1. **Formal Cause**: The design or essence that makes a thing what it is. 
2. **Final Cause**: The purpose or end for which it was made. 
3. **Efficient Cause**: The maker or agent that brought it into being. 
4. **Material Cause**: The physical matter from which the thing is made.</note>

—pretensions of knowing matters that are beyond human comprehension and imagination, while they actually possess no knowledge of them. For instance, some of them bother themselves with measuring the sizes and distances of the heavenly bodies; some are obsessed with determining the heights of mountains and clouds; many wander about measuring the surface area of deserts and seas; and some are busy trying to discover the structure of the heavens and the center¹ of the earth—all while remaining ignorant of the structure and size of their own bodies! 

They do not know how many intestines² they have; what the capacity of the chest cavity <term>thoracic cavity</term> is; the condition of the heart and brain; how the stomach is formed; how the bones are shaped; or how the joints of the body are positioned. Things like these—which are easy to understand and necessary to know—they are completely ignorant of, even though God’s skill and wondrous power are seen through them. As the Prophet (may God bless him and grant him peace) declared: "He who knows himself has attained knowledge of his Lord." And besides their ignorance in—

They order the patient to abstain from the very things that the sick often find relief in. Whereas, if they were to let nature take its course, the sick would recover.

# CHAPTER XXIV.

### CONTAINING AN ACCOUNT OF THE WHITE ANT.

When the parrot had finished this speech, the king looked toward the assembly of the Jinn <note>original: "Genii"</note> and humans and said: “How does the white ant <note>A termite.</note>, despite having no hands or legs, lift soil and build its arched structure over itself? Explain this matter to me.” 

A man of the Hebrew people <margin>The <term>Urdu</term> translator seems to have forgotten that there was but one <term>Hebrew</term> present. “The representative of the Hebrews said” <note>original Urdu: "Ibrāniyon ke wakīl ne kahā"</note> should have been the <term>Urdu</term> phrasing.</margin> said: “The Jinn carry the soil to this insect because she performed a kind act for them. Specifically <note>original: "viz."</note>, she ate through the staff <margin>See the <term>Quran</term> <note>original: "Korān"</note>, chapter 34 (<term>Saba</term>), verse 14; and the note on this passage in Sale’s Translation.</margin> of the honored Solomon, and he fell down. This was how the Jinn finally knew that he was dead; they fled from that place and obtained relief from their hard labor and suffering.” 

<note>According to legend, King Solomon died while standing in prayer, leaning on his staff. Because the Jinn were terrified of him, they continued their forced labor for a long time, not realizing he was dead until a termite ate through the wood of his staff, causing his body to fall.</note>

The king said to the scholars <note>original: "doctors"</note> of the Jinn: “Do you have any knowledge of the circumstance which this man relates?” 

They all replied, saying: “How can we confirm that the Jinn carry soil and water to it?"

<summary>The King of the Jinn asks how termites are able to build complex structures without limbs, leading a Hebrew representative to cite the legend of Solomon's death to suggest the Jinn assist the insects out of gratitude.</summary>
<keywords>white ant, termite, Jinn, Solomon, Quran, architecture, insect behavior, Hebrew representative</keywords>

<language>English</language>

->**THE BRETHREN OF PURITY**<-

“For if it [the termite] had performed the act of kindness¹ for the Jinn <note>original: "Genii"</note> that this man describes, they would still be trapped in the same hard labor and trouble and would not have escaped. After all, the honored Solomon also used to force them to carry earth and water to build structures, and he didn't impose any other kind of suffering on them.”

The Greek sage said to the king, “I am familiar with the reason for this.” 

“Tell us,” the king replied. 

He continued, saying: “The white ant <note>A termite.</note> has a strange and wondrous anatomy. Her physical nature <note>original: "constitution"</note> is extremely cold, and her entire body is covered with tiny gaps and pores that always remain open. Because of this intense cold, the air that enters her body condenses into water. This moisture then oozes <note>original: "exudes"</note>² onto the surface of her body. The dust that falls on her body then collects there, turning into mud. She gathers this mud and uses it to build a structure over herself for shelter, so that she can remain safe from all harm. Furthermore, she has a pair of extremely sharp mandibles <note>original: "lips"</note> which she uses to cut through fruit, grain, plants, and wood, and even to bore through brick and stone.”

***

¹ <note>The commentator notes that the reasoning in the <term>Urdu</term> translation is logically flawed. The original <term>Arabic</term> text says: “They said, 'We cannot understand why the Jinn would do this; because if they are carrying this earth and water, then they are still in a state of disgraceful trouble. Solomon forced them to do nothing but carry mud and water for the building of his cities.'”</note>

² <note>The translator notes that the <term>Urdu</term> word *tapaktā* (dripping) is inappropriate here; *sījhtā* (oozing or sweating) is the more accurate translation of the original <term>Arabic</term> word.</note>

The king said to the cricket: “The white ant <note>A termite.</note> is a species of reptile <note>original: "reptile"; in historical natural history, insects and crawling creatures were often classified together as reptiles.</note>, and you are the representative of the reptiles; explain, then, what it is that this Greek sage says.” 

The cricket answered, saying: “He speaks the truth; but he has not given a complete description of this insect; something has been omitted.” 

The king said: “Then you complete it.” 

He continued: “When God (may He be exalted) created all the tribes of animals and bestowed His gifts upon every tribe, in His wisdom and justice, He placed them all on an equal level. To some He gave large bodies and powerful frames, but made their minds very weak and submissive; and to some He gave small and feeble bodies, but made their minds very intelligent and sharp. In this way, the abundance and the deficiency on both sides are perfectly balanced. 

For example: the elephant, despite his huge body, is so weak-spirited <note>original: "contemptible of soul"</note> that he submits to a boy who climbs onto his shoulders and drives him wherever he pleases. And the camel, although he has a large body and a long neck, is yet so dull-witted that he follows anyone who takes hold of his lead-rope <note>original: "nose-string"</note>. Even a mouse could lead him about if it wished. 

In contrast, the scorpion, despite its small body, destroys even an elephant when it stings him. In the same way, this insect which is called the white ant, although very small of body, is very strong of mind. In short, all the animals that have small bodies are intelligent and wise.” The

***

The king asked the reason for this, saying: “Why are large-bodied animals stupid, while small-bodied ones are intelligent? Where is God’s wisdom seen in this?”

He replied, saying: “When the Creator, in His omnipotence <note>Unlimited power.</note>, saw that animals with large bodies are suited for labor and hard work, He knew that if He gave them sharp minds, they would never submit to anyone. On the other hand, if small-bodied creatures were not intelligent and wise, they would constantly be in trouble and suffering.¹ On this account, He gave the former weak spirits and the latter intelligent spirits.”

“Explain this in detail,” said the king.

He continued, saying: “The beauty of every work of art consists of this: that the skill of the craftsman is not obvious to anyone, nor is it clear how he performs it. For example, the bee—without a ruler or compass—makes various kinds of angles and circles² in her cells, and no one knows how she makes them, or where she brings her wax and honey from. If her body were large, her skill would be obvious. The situation of the silkworm is similar, for no one understands how she spins and weaves. The same is true for the white ant <note>A termite.</note>; nothing...”

<meta>This page continues the debate between humans and animals before the King of the Jinn. It follows a speech by the cricket and moves into a philosophical argument regarding unity and diversity as evidence of superiority.</meta>

<header>->**THE BRETHREN OF PURITY**<- <note>original: "IKHWANU-S-SAFA"</note></header>

When the cricket had finished this speech, the king said to the men: “Now, do you have anything else to say?” They replied, “We still have many noble <note>original: "eminent"</note> gifts left to mention, by which it is proven that we are the masters and these are our slaves.” 

“Tell us what they are,” said the king. 

One of the men said, “Our physical forms are identical <note>original: "one and the same"</note>, while theirs are varied. From this, it is clear that we are the masters and they are the slaves; for leadership and authority <note>original: "master-ship"</note> are best associated with unity <note>original: "oneness"</note>, while servitude is associated with diversity <note>original: "multiplicity"</note>.” 

The king asked the animals, “What answer do you give to this?” 

For a moment, all the animals bowed their heads in thought. Then the nightingale, the representative <note>original: "deputy"</note> of the birds, said: “This man speaks the truth; however, although animals have various shapes, the minds of all of them are as one. On the other hand, although men share one common form, their minds are extremely different.” 

“What is the proof of this?” asked the king. 

The nightingale replied, “The diversity of religions and creeds among them shows this. There are thousands of religious groups among them—such as Jews, Christians, Magians <note>term: Zoroastrians</note>, polytheists, non-believers <note>original: "infidels"</note>, idolaters, fire-worshippers, and star-worshippers. Furthermore, each religion is divided into numerous sects; just as among the ancient philosophers <note>original: "sages"</note>, each one held opinions different from all the rest. For example, among the Jews there are Samaritans, ‘Abalis,¹ and—”
